# Decisions, Caveats & Known Issues

Durable memory across Claude Code sessions. Read this before proposing architecture or
diagnosing behaviour that looks like a bug.

**Rules for writing here — this file must stay scannable:**
- One row per entry. No prose paragraphs, no code blocks. Cite `file.py:line` instead of restating code.
- Record only what the code and git history cannot tell you: *why* a choice was made, what was
  rejected, what is deliberately unfinished. Never document what a function does.
- Add a row when you decide something non-obvious, discover a landmine, or find earlier
  analysis was wrong. Do not add a row for routine work.
- **Delete rows that are resolved or obsolete.** Growth is the failure mode. Update in place
  rather than appending a correction.
- New IDs continue the sequence; never renumber.

---

## Decisions

| ID | Date | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| D-422 | 2026-09-16 | **The screen's own inputs reach the session, because three of them did not** — `appTestingCommand` emits `--project` and `--attest` and shows the requirement as the file the command reads; `explore_app.py` takes `--attest` instead of hardcoding `True`. | The command said `--requirement-file requirement.txt` and nothing ever produced that file, so a first run died on a missing path and the typed requirement reached nothing. It named no project, so the session was filed under none, every `--cover-review` came back as "no such run" because it belonged to a project the session could not see, and the project's documents were never read. All three failures are silent. Between them they made D-419's compulsory basis **unreachable from the UI**: a person could fill the screen in exactly as designed and get a session with none of the three sources arriving. The attestation is the same error one level up — the driver declared a test environment on the caller's behalf, which is nobody's declaration; the tick box is now the record and the platform refuses a session without it. |
| D-423 | 2026-09-16 | **A stop is served as the existing `done` reply, checked before `require_open` and before metering** — `stop_requested_at` on `app_sessions`, `POST /sessions/{id}/request-stop`, collected at the top of `take_step`. | The browser runs on the customer's machine and this platform cannot reach it (D-407), but the driver asks for an action every step, so the *reply* is the channel. `done` predates this feature and every driver already honours it, so there is no version-skew matrix and no change to `explore_app.py` at all. Three properties follow from the ordering and none is incidental: before `require_open`, because that raises a 400 and the driver turns any status >= 400 into `SystemExit`, losing the steps already paid for, where `done` exits cleanly and still calls `/finish` and `/generate`; before `guarded_call`, so the stopping step makes no model call; and the session row is *closed*, so enforcement is server-side rather than a request the driver may ignore. **Rejected: holding the step response until a human answers** — the step is a synchronous POST with a 180-second client timeout, so blocking it on a person is a guaranteed timeout mid-action, and it needs a driver change. **Rejected: the platform reaching the driver** — it is usually behind a VPN with no listening port, and it would create the inbound surface D-407 exists to avoid. What it cannot do goes on the label: it lands at a step boundary and cannot close the browser window, so the button reads *Stop after the current step*. |
| D-424 | 2026-09-16 | **The loop detector lives in the browser, as an observation** — `renderAppStalls` in `frontend/app.js`. | `app_session.stalled` fires only when the last five page signatures are *identical*, so an A,B,A,B loop never trips it — which is exactly the live session that burned all twenty-five of its steps moving between two pages, while that function's own docstring claimed "a genuine two-page loop still counts as no progress". Fixing the server-side rule changes exploration behaviour and would move D-411's measured floors, so it is separate, measured work; showing the loop costs nothing and is what a person needs in the moment. Every line is phrased as an observation and never a verdict, because the evidence is **n=1**: a wizard that legitimately revisits a page trips the same rule, and a page carrying a clock never repeats a signature at all, so the countdown never advances. |
| D-425 | 2026-09-16 | **An abandoned session is closed on a clock, and its digest then expires on the window its customer chose** — `app_session.reap_abandoned` (6 hours) at startup and on the session list, then `sweep_expired_page_text` judging each abandoned session against its own `page_text_retention_days` from `started_at`. | D-418 promised that closing the browser half way through was not a way of keeping the digest indefinitely, and the SQL under that sentence read `status != 'exploring'` — which excludes the only case the sentence describes, because an abandoned session stays at `exploring` for ever. The promise did not hold. Reaping is what moves the status and lets the existing sweep see it, which is most of why it is worth building rather than merely labelling. The window is the **customer's choice** rather than a fixed 90-day backstop, and zero is honoured: for a session that will never write a suite, "no longer than necessary" ends when the session closes. That costs the ability to write a suite later from the steps it did take, so the screen says so where the choice is made. The reap deliberately does **not** start the retention clock — at zero days that would wipe the digest at the moment of reaping and destroy the recovery path for every session, not only the ones that asked for it. Six hours is generous on purpose: reaping early makes `require_open` refuse a *live* driver's next step and kills a session that was working. |
| D-429 | 2026-09-16 | **A project can permit a control the denylist withholds, by exact name, and the permitted list is frozen onto the session** — `app_allowances`, `action_policy.screen(candidates, allow)`, returned to the driver in the open response's `policy.permitted`. | The list reads button labels with no context, so it cannot tell "Remove [from basket]" from "Remove [user]" and nothing it could be taught would let it. Measured: on ParaBank it withheld every money movement in three steps, on SauceDemo it withheld `Remove` and `Checkout` and left six of fifteen supplied requirements untestable. Four properties make it safe: matching is **exact and normalised, never a substring** (a substring list would let "delete" permit "Delete account", this module's failure reached from the other side); the validity checks survive it, because disabled and non-interactive are about whether an element can be driven rather than whether it is safe; it is scoped to one project; and permitting something not being withheld is refused, since a list that accepts anything teaches people it does nothing. **The hard part is that the index stops being safe.** The platform screens to choose an index and the driver screens again to resolve it, which only means anything while both lists are identical — true by construction until this gave the two sides different rules. Hence the freeze at open, the list travelling to the driver rather than being looked up, and `permits` cross-checking the chosen name against the position. Two defects fell out of building it: `permits` never made that name check despite its docstring implying one, and the server's own re-check ran without the allow-list, refusing exactly what `screen` had offered one line earlier. |
| D-430 | 2026-09-16 | **The suite writer is told what each action *did*, taken from the step that reported it** — `_outcomes_by_step` in `backend/routes/app_testing.py`, used by `_journeys_markdown` and `_history`. | The same off-by-one as D-428, in the place where it costs money rather than credibility. `_journeys_markdown` printed `— observed: {step['outcome']}` against the step the column sits on, so the first live session told the model that step 3, **click Login**, resulted in `filled Password; still on /`. Signing in, as far as the generation input was concerned, went nowhere. The model then invented a landing condition for the sign-in helper — `getByRole('heading', { name: 'Products' })`, a role that page does not have — and **every test in the suite failed on that one line**. Grouping is by journey and the reporting step is the next one *chronologically*, so the pairing is computed before the grouping, not inside it. |
| D-431 | 2026-09-16 | **Page text is collected from the element that renders the words, not from whatever carries an `id`** — `TEXTS_JS` in `scripts/explore_app.py`: landmarks always, otherwise a leaf, inline markup not counting as a child, candidates excluded by their `data-lm-cand` stamp, 120 characters a line, 28 lines. | The old selector asked whether an element carries an id, which is a habit of one era of front-end code rather than a fact about where words live. On SauceDemo's catalogue **34 elements matched and not one was a heading** — the application has no `h1`-`h6` at all — so every match was a container and the single line collected was the navigation run together. Six product names and six prices, which are what the supplied requirements were about, were never sent; the suite asserted them anyway through `.inventory_item_price`, recalled rather than observed, which works on a famous application and will not on a customer's. Four details are load-bearing: inline markup does not make a parent a container, or `<h1>Order <span>#12</span></h1>` shatters; candidates are excluded by the stamp `COLLECT_JS` already leaves, which is exact where a tag list is a guess, and matters because a `<li>` navigation is a candidate (D-414); the line limit dropped 200 → 120, because with the leaf rule the long lines are genuine prose rather than run-together containers and three product descriptions filled the budget before the last two prices; and the cap rose 18 → 28 **on both sides**, since `app_session.clip_texts` silently discards anything above its own. |
| D-433 | 2026-09-17 | **A locator the session never observed is counted in code, handed back once, and reported on the suite** — `backend/services/locator_audit.py`, the retry in `app_explorer_agent.generate_suite`, the counts in `orchestrator.generate_app_suite`, `_appLocatorPanel` on the screen. | The prompt has said *never write a CSS selector* since the first version and a live suite contained nineteen of them, three matching nothing — so repeating the rule was not a fix, and this is the part that does not depend on the model agreeing. What makes it checkable rather than a matter of taste is that the observation contract is **closed**: `COLLECT_JS` emits a role locator, a text locator, or `locator("input[name='…']")`, and nothing else, so a class, an id or a test id in the output was recalled from another application by construction. The line is drawn at **application-specific** tokens, not at CSS: a bare `tbody tr` used to narrow to a row before asserting inside it is what the prompt itself asks for, and flagging it would make the checker cry wolf in the same way the suites do. `getByTestId` counts as invented although it looks like best practice, because the session was never shown a test id either — calling it grounded would be the flattering-number error. Three guards on the retry, each against a way it could cost more than it returns: never after the empty-files retry, so two generation calls stays the ceiling `agent_guard` reserves for; only accept a strictly cleaner answer; and only accept one that kept as many files *and* as many tests, because the cheapest way to stop using class selectors is to delete the tests that used them, which would trade a false alarm for a coverage hole and score perfectly. Nothing is rewritten — mechanically editing generated test code is how a working file becomes a broken one — so a suite that still carries one ships with the count and a sentence telling the reader to suspect the test before the application. |
| D-432 | 2026-09-16 | **A run that fails everywhere it does the same setup step says so** — `_shared_cause` in `scripts/run_tests.py`, read off the local JUnit. | Both live runs failed as one wrong locator in the shared sign-in helper, 18 of 18 and then 14 of 17. An all-red suite is precisely what a broken application looks like, so the reader concludes their app is down when the tests could not get past the login form — and red is the signal this product exists to produce, so a false alarm devalues every later one. Nothing else could say it: the platform never runs what it writes (D-05), and the per-requirement reconciliation groups by requirement, where one broken helper reads as fifteen unrelated failures. Reported only above two thirds of failures and never for a single one, because sending somebody after a helper that is fine costs more than staying quiet. The frame pattern stops the file group at a bracket rather than at whitespace: a Windows home directory has a space in it and the first version matched nothing on the machine that produced the report. |
| D-427 | 2026-09-16 | **A candidate's name is the name the accessibility tree computes, and a name that is not one never becomes a role locator** — `COLLECT_JS` in `scripts/explore_app.py` reads `value` for `<input type=submit|button|reset>`, and `nameIsAccessible` decides between `getByRole(...)` and an attribute selector. | The first two live sessions against public applications each produced a suite that could not run **one** test. The name chain never looked at `value` and fell through to the `name` *attribute*, which is a form field name and never an accessible name: SauceDemo's `<input type="submit" id="login-button" name="login-button" value="Login">` was offered as `login-button`, the suite was written against `getByRole('button', { name: 'login-button' })`, and all 18 tests timed out on that one line in the shared `signIn` helper without reaching a single assertion. ParaBank's login button carries no `name` attribute at all, so it was offered with an empty name and the suite got `getByRole('button', { name: '' })`. The string was present the whole time, in the `value` field, where it meant "what somebody typed here" — which is also why a button now reports no value: read as an entry it says the form is already filled. This is D-415's own reasoning one field across: that row got the *role* right — never claim a role a non-semantic element lacks — and left the *name* assumed. Nothing offline could see it because every control in the fixture and in all three eval cases is a semantic `<button>`; `--fixture` now carries all three `<input type=submit>` shapes and asserts that Playwright resolves each to exactly one element, since a name that looks plausible and resolves to nothing is the whole failure. |
| D-428 | 2026-09-16 | **A step's `outcome` column describes the step *before* it, and the live view pairs them accordingly** — `_appOutcomeOf` in `frontend/app.js`. | An outcome can only be known by looking at the page again, so the driver sends `last_outcome` with the *following* request and the route stores it on the step it is creating (`app_testing.py:192` names the field in as many words). Joining the two on one row states something false, and the first real session rendered it: `click “Finish” on /checkout-step-two.html → navigated to /checkout-step-two.html`, crediting Finish with arriving at the page it was standing on in order to click Finish. Clicking *continue*, one step earlier, is what went there. The newest step now says its result has not been reported rather than showing nothing, because a blank reads as "the action did nothing". Left alone deliberately: the failed-step count, which reads the same strings whichever row they sit on. |
| D-426 | 2026-09-16 | **What the policy withheld is recorded by name, and survives the page-text wipe** — `refused_summary` on `app_sessions`, `action_policy.classify_detail` supplying the group, rendered on the live view and on the finished suite. | The counts were always kept and the names always discarded, so the session record could say "it declined 14 controls" and not which — and *which* is the half an operator needs, because a denylist eating half of every page is a **coverage** problem rather than a safety win and nothing could show it. Bounded by distinct name (40), so a loop meeting the same button on every page stores one entry with a count rather than forty rows. Deliberately outside `wipe_page_text`, which promises a person can still see what a session refused after the digest is gone. The names are page-controlled text of the same class as `action.target_name`, already stored unconditionally and already sent to the model. |
| D-421 | 2026-09-15 | **A suite generation that comes back with no files is retried once, and the retry is reported** — `_has_files` and the retry in `app_explorer_agent.generate_suite`, surfaced as `generation_retried`. | Observed on roughly one session in three for `signup-wizard`, and it is **not truncation**: the call ends `stop_reason=tool_use` with every other field populated — a confident summary, assertion rows describing tests — and `test_files` empty. What makes it worth recovering rather than merely refusing (D-417) is who pays: the failure lands *after* the whole exploration, so every step the customer already bought produces nothing and the only remedy is to explore again from scratch. One retry costs one generation call against a session's worth of steps. Reported rather than hidden, because a silent retry turns a one-in-three defect into a one-in-nine defect nobody ever measures — the eval would stop seeing it and the cause would never be found. The cause is still unknown, which is why `_refuse_degenerate` now logs the keys the model returned and the raw shape of `test_files`: an intermittent fault whose evidence is gone by the time anybody asks costs a paid run to reproduce. |
| D-420 | 2026-09-15 | **An assertion says whose word it rests on: `stated`, `derived` or `observed`** — `ASSERTION_BASES` in `app_explorer_agent`, ceiling enforced in `orchestrator.generate_app_suite`. Replaces the two-value `observed`/`specified`. | D-409 with the middle rung it turned out to need. The test-case agent produces a near-perfect brief for this product — numbered steps, an expected result on each — so using it is obviously right; treating it as a *specification* is obviously wrong, because a machine wrote it from somebody's requirement and nobody has checked it. Without a third word the choice was to either refuse good material or let a model's guess be laundered into "we verified your requirement" in two hops, which is D-406's rule (a model's claim about its own output is not evidence) at one remove. So `stated` means a person wrote it — a typed requirement or a project document; `derived` means this platform generated it; `observed` means nothing said it. Enforced as a **ceiling** rather than a correction: what the session actually had decides the strongest label any row may carry, and anything above it is pushed down with the reason recorded on the row. What this cannot do is verify that a `derived` row really traces to the case it names — it only stops a claim the session could not support at all. `specified_count` is still emitted under its old name, because the renderer, the smoke checks and the eval all read it and a count that silently becomes zero reads as a collapse in quality rather than a rename. |
| D-419 | 2026-09-15 | **A session will not start without something to check the application against** — `services/app_basis.py`, refused in `app_session.create` beside the attestation. Three sources, any one of which is enough: a typed requirement, a Requirements or QA **document** on the project, or a finished Test Scenario / Test Case run to cover. | The measured consequence of not having this: a live session against this platform's own interface, opened with nothing supplied, produced eleven tests of which **nine** were some form of "the button is visible" — green, confident, and passing just as happily when the feature behind the button is broken. The product's whole value is the difference between a test that checks behaviour and one that checks furniture, and that difference is decided entirely by whether anything says what the behaviour should be. Refused at the door rather than discovered afterwards, because the alternative costs a whole paid session plus a suite somebody has to be talked out of trusting. **Documents were already wired and already useless**: the knowledge base is per-project and per-category and `generate_app_suite` already read the `qa` shelf, but only a *typed* requirement counted as a basis — so a customer could upload a full specification and still have every assertion downgraded. `DOC_CATEGORIES` is `("requirement", "qa")` and deliberately not everything: a specification is filed under Requirements and is the most useful document a test writer can have, while a deployment runbook in front of the model makes the three relevant paragraphs harder to find. Prior runs are **optional and usually absent**, which is the point — they are a stronger brief when they exist, not a prerequisite. |
| D-418 | 2026-09-15 | **The page-text digest is encrypted, expires, and can be refused outright** — `app_session._seal`/`_unseal`, `page_text_retention_days` (0-90, default 7), `collect_page_text`, swept at startup and on the session read path. **Resolves C-98.** | D-416 made this platform hold something it had not held before: not button labels but what a customer's pages *display*, which on a test environment seeded from production is their data. The answer is the one already used for customer source (`review_source_store`) rather than a new mechanism — Fernet at rest, a retention window, and a sweep that runs without a scheduler because this deployment has none. **Zero days is a real setting and not "off"**: after the suite is written nothing needs the digest, so wiping it at that moment costs the customer nothing but the ability to look back at why a test was written, which makes it the tightest honest position. The clock starts at generation rather than at `finish` for that reason, and an abandoned session is swept from its start time so that closing the browser is not a way to keep the digest for ever. Refusing it entirely is also supported and was the user's explicit call over my objection; the objection is answered by making the consequence loud rather than by removing the choice — the screen states it at the point of choosing and `page_text_note` states it on the finished suite, because the failure mode of a quiet privacy switch is a customer who turns it on, gets presence-only tests, and blames the product. Enforced on **both** sides: the driver stops sending and the route drops what arrives, since a setting one side can defeat is not a setting. |
| D-417 | 2026-09-15 | **For a generator whose deliverable is files, a summary does not rescue an empty result** — `_refuse_degenerate(..., require_payload=True)` in `orchestrator.py`, passed by `generate_test_automation` and `generate_app_suite`. | D-405's rule — empty payload *and* empty summary is a malformed call — is right for a catalogue: a model that found nothing to enumerate says so, and the reader has the sentence explaining why. It is wrong for a suite, where the files *are* the deliverable and no sentence substitutes for them. A live eval run produced exactly the shape the old rule waves through: zero `test_files`, three `assertion_basis` rows describing tests that did not exist, and a confident summary claiming two journeys had been covered. It was stored as a successful run, and `run_tests.py` would have found nothing to execute — the "green suite that proves nothing" of C-66, with no suite at all. Applied to the code-based generator as well as the new one, because it has the identical hole; nothing that produced files is affected, so no measured baseline moves. |
| D-416 | 2026-09-15 | **An observation is what a page offers *and* what it says** — `TEXTS_JS` in `scripts/explore_app.py`, `StepRequest.page_texts`, stored on `app_steps.page_texts` and rendered into the journey record as `page showed:`. | The explorer originally saw only interactive elements, which meant a suite could assert *which controls exist* and nothing about what the application did — and a basket whose total is wrong offers exactly the same controls as one whose total is right. So "assert the behaviour, not that a page loaded" was being demanded of a model that could not see any behaviour. Landmarks only (headings, live regions, short identified text), each line prefixed with how to address it (`heading:`, `status:`, `text:`), capped at `MAX_PAGE_TEXTS`/`MAX_PAGE_TEXT_CHARS` **on arrival** rather than trusted from the driver, because it is model input whose size a customer's page decides. It is also folded into `page_signature`: on a client-rendered page the text is the only thing that changes when something happens, and a signature blind to it calls a working session stalled. The cost is the opposite failure — a page with a clock never repeats a signature — which is the better one to have, since `max_steps` still bounds the session while a premature stop wastes all of it. |
| D-415 | 2026-09-15 | **The driver works out how to address each control; the model never does** — `locator` and `submits` on every candidate, computed in the page, sanitised on arrival (`_locator`, `_submit_target` in `routes/app_testing.py`). | Two things the model was being asked to invent, both now read off the DOM. **The locator**: `getByRole` resolves through the accessibility tree, so it finds a `<button>` and does *not* find an `<li>` that merely behaves like one — claiming a role for a non-semantic element yields a locator matching nothing, which is worse than a plain text match, so those get `getByText` and say so. **The submit target**: a test that "the server refuses this" needs the request the working form sends, and on a correct application the forbidden form is simply absent, so there is nothing to read it from at replay time. Both are sanitised because they are page-controlled strings heading into a prompt: a submit target must be `METHOD /path` (an absolute URL would carry the origin D-407 says this side never learns) and a locator must be a `getBy…` call. Neither is ever executed here — an action is performed against the stamped candidate, never against a string — so they are advisory text, and D-408 is intact: the model still returns an index. |
| D-414 | 2026-09-15 | **An element the application made clickable is a candidate even when it is not a control** — the second pass in `COLLECT_JS`, on `onclick`, `tabindex`, or a pointer cursor. | Found by the first live run against a real application, and it is the difference between the product working outside a corpus and not. After signing in, this platform's own interface offered the explorer **two** candidates: all forty of its navigation items are `<li data-page=…>` with a click handler and no role, invisible to a selector that looks for controls. With nothing left to click it spent twenty-two of twenty-five steps guessing URLs — rational behaviour given what it was shown, and a wasted session. There is no way to ask the DOM whether something has a listener, so the pass uses the signals that are observable, which are the same ones a person uses. Three rules keep it from flooding: the **outermost** clickable wins (a nav item's icon `<span>` inherits the pointer cursor and reads as a second control called nothing but an emoji), a wrapper containing a real control is skipped in favour of the control, and a label with no letters or digits is dropped as unaddressable. **Unmeasurable by the eval** — all three corpus applications are ordinary semantic HTML — so the evidence is the fixture and the live run, and that gap is C-97. |
| D-413 | 2026-09-15 | **A password field reports that it holds a value, never what the value is** — `COLLECT_JS` emits the fixed marker `(a value has been entered)`; `perform()` reports what a fill did rather than only that the page did not navigate. | Reporting the field as empty no matter what was the right instinct and the wrong result: an explorer that has just typed a password sees a page identical to the one before, concludes the fill did not happen, and types it again. The first live run against a real application typed the password four times, never pressed Sign In, and was stopped by the no-progress rule having never got past the login form — and the same loop then appeared in the corpus, so it was intermittent rather than app-specific. Since almost everything worth testing is behind a login, this made the product's main claim unreachable. A fixed marker discloses nothing, not even a length. Two supporting changes: `perform()` now answers "filled Password; still on /" instead of "stayed on the same page", which was true about navigation and silent about whether the action worked; and `page_signature` includes each control's **value**, because filling in a form is progress that changes nothing else — without it a form with more fields than `NO_PROGRESS_LIMIT` is stopped part way through being filled in and the button at the bottom is never reached. Typing the *same* thing again still produces the same signature, which is the behaviour the rule exists to catch. |
| D-412 | 2026-09-15 | **An expected output can never exceed the per-call ceiling, and calibration by product id is invalid for a product whose calls have two shapes** — `for_chunks` clamps to `max_out`; `CALIBRATION_BY_PRODUCT_IS_INVALID` and `MEASURED_OUTPUT_TOKENS_PER_CALL` in `services/review_estimate.py`. | A live 25-step session quoted **"$1.31 expected, $1.18 ceiling"**, which is not a quote. The forecast and the bound come from different places — history or a default, against the declared cap — and nothing stopped the forecast passing the bound it is supposed to sit under: `app-testing-step` caps a call at 1,500 tokens while the default forecast is 3,000, so every step was priced for twice the output the request permits. It is the only agent whose declared cap sits below the default, so the clamp moves no other product's quote. The second half is subtler and would have survived the clamp: `get_output_calibration` is keyed on **product id**, and `app-testing` bills a session's N small step decisions and its one large generation under one product — so the median over its rows is the *step* median, and pricing the suite call off it would under-quote that call roughly thirty-fold, while `app-testing-step` is not a product id at all and can only ever come back empty. Both now use numbers measured over live runs against three applications (n=45 steps, n=5 generations), set above p90 rather than at the median because landing under actual is the direction that matters in a quote. Calibration by product is sound exactly when one product makes one kind of call. The quote for a 25-step session went from $1.31 to $0.33 against a measured actual near $0.20. |
| D-411 | 2026-09-15 | **Application-based testing is measured by exploring the working app and replaying the suite against a broken one** — `scripts/eval_app_testing.py`, `evals/app_testing/<case>/{source,fixed}` where each variant is a *runnable web application*. Headline `discriminates`: green on fixed **and** red on broken. | Discovery runs against the **fixed** application, which is the opposite of D-403's corpus and is deliberate: a customer explores the system they have and wants a suite that guards it, so the claim to measure is regression-catching, not bug-finding. With no specification a suite cannot find a pre-existing bug, and an eval scoring it as though it could would measure a promise the product does not make. **First measured 2026-09-15 on the one case that then existed: `discriminates` 33%** — in two runs of three exactly one test also failed on the *working* application, which is why `detects_break` exists as a separate number (the agent saw the regression every time and was not clean on the build it was written from). **Re-measured the same day after the work that finding prompted, PROMPT_VERSION 2026-09-15.3, three cases x three runs: `discriminates` 8 of 9 at $0.144 a session, with no test failing on the working application in any session that produced one.** The one miss is not a poor suite but *no* suite — a generation returning a confident summary and an empty `test_files` (D-421), measured before the retry that now recovers it. The denominator is every session **attempted**: counting only the sessions that produced something would have reported 8 of 8, which is the scoreboard committing the flattering-number error this eval exists to catch. The distance between the two numbers is real work rather than a prompt tidy-up, and each step of it was found by a run: a negative assertion driving controls that exist only on the broken build; an explorer that could see no page text and therefore could not assert a value (D-416); a login it could not get past (D-413); a cleanup hook that hung; and a suite of zero files stored as a success (D-417). Floors are now set at 0.66 on all three measures for all three cases — one run below a measured 100%, which is a gate, where one run below 33% is zero and gates nothing. | D-384 | 2026-09-10 | **A codebase too large to send whole sends the files the requirement is about, not nothing** — `input_mode: hybrid`, ranked deterministically by `test_context_brief.select_files`, quoted verbatim beside `code_brief`'s map of everything else. | The old choice was binary: all files or none. Over the threshold a 300,000-character repository sent nothing verbatim, so the agent guessed exactly the route paths, signatures and fixture names a test asserts on — D-316's failure mode arriving as "all the context and no code" rather than "one file and no context". Whole files only: a model cannot tell it is reading half of something, so it reasons about the missing half as though it had seen it. The ranking is a heuristic and is **shown** — `input_selection` carries every pick and its reason on the free preflight — because a ranking nobody can inspect is a ranking nobody can correct. Costs more than a description alone and less than the whole codebase; `focus` is the lever for a caller who wants the saving on a codebase that would have fit. |
| D-382 | 2026-09-09 | **A JavaScript project's test runner is read from `package.json`, not inferred from imports** — `test_context_brief.declared_frameworks`, reported separately from the import-derived counts and labelled as the different claim it is. | Found by running the brief against a real Jest repository, not by reading it: `describe`, `it` and `expect` are **globals** under Jest, Mocha and Vitest, so an ordinary suite imports nothing any text marker can see. The brief said "no framework is confirmed" for a project whose manifest plainly declared `jest`. A manifest entry is exact where a text pattern is a guess, so the two are kept apart rather than summed. The live run still produced correct Jest+supertest tests because the verbatim exemplar carried the idiom — which is the argument for D-378's design, not a reason to leave this unfixed. |
| D-377 | 2026-09-09 | **Test automation is a new product, not a wider `test-case`** — `test-automation` (`agents/qa_intelligence/test_automation_agent.py`), the only QA product inheriting `_CodeSourceBase`. | Generation-from-prose and generation-from-code are different jobs with different costs. Extending `test-case` would force a cost quote onto every run that does not need one, and change a shipped product's response shape. Phase 1 of a three-phase plan; phases 2-3 (a JUnit-XML results contract, then CI/local producers) are not built, and video capture plus test-management push are deliberately deferred past them. |
| D-378 | 2026-09-09 | **A generated suite is shown what the project's own tests look like, not told about them** — `services/test_context_brief.py` finds the tests, framework, runner config, fixtures and where the client is built, and **quotes one or two real test files verbatim**. A test file has to know the import path, the fixture that builds the app, the auth helper and the client idiom; told none of them a model invents all four and the file fails at collection time, proving nothing. Showing a test that already works beats describing the conventions. Four defects found by running it before wiring it, all fixed: the client idiom was blank for a textbook pytest project (only test files were scanned, and the client lives in `conftest.py`); the unit test outranked the integration test as exemplar; bare framework names matched **this module's own marker table** and reported a Python/SQLite app as using Cypress and RSpec; and `class Test<Name>` plus a name-only match judged the two QA agents to be the project's test suite. | D-379 | 2026-09-09 | **The codebase reaches this agent in one of two modes, and the mode is reported** — `test_context_brief.for_automation` sends the **real source** under 220,000 chars and `code_brief`'s structural description over it; `input_mode` is on the free quote and on the result. | A test must name a real function signature and a dependency graph does not carry one, so `code_brief` alone is weaker here than it is for an architecture review. Both are legitimate; what is not is letting a reader trust them equally. The same reasoning makes `assumptions` (each with `risk_if_wrong`) and `not_automated` **required** schema fields rather than optional ones. |
| D-373 | 2026-09-09 | **The four remaining code-reading intakes, each in the shape its agent's question has** — Migration Assessment joins Architecture and Security on the structural brief (`/api/review/migration-assessment/{source,upload-zip,preflight}`); 21 CFR Part 11 and GxP Generation get the **real files** through `main._regulated_files` (`/{source,preflight}` each, plus the GxP screen's first-ever `/preflight-zip`); Code Generation gets neither and instead reads `POST /api/code-brief` into the context box a person can see. | Four calls, one per intake. (1) **Migration Assessment is a holistic question**, not a fan-out: `MIGRATION_ASSESSMENT_TOOL_SCHEMA` returns one `complexity` and one `estimated_effort` the way `ARCHITECTURE_TOOL_SCHEMA` returns one `quality_scores`, so twenty partial answers have no merge rule — same reasoning as D-370, and it reuses `_design_review_from_source` unchanged. Its language caveat is the sharpest on the platform and the screen says so: migration is most wanted for the oldest stack in the building, and **COBOL and RPG have no parser**. (2) **The regulated pair get files, not a description** — "is there an audit-trail write when a record changes" is a question about code and a summary cannot answer it. Part 11 splits when the prompt does not fit (D-354) so it is in `_CHUNKABLE_ROUTES`; `gxp_generator_agent.plan` does not split, so it is deliberately not, because over-reserving denies calls that would have succeeded. (3) **Code Generation's brief is a separate, free, visible step, not a new prompt field.** A prompt change is the one kind with no eval here to catch a regression (D-344), so a run with no repository stays byte-identical; and a computed description of somebody's codebase should be readable before it is sent. (4) **`migration_target` is optional on the model and required by the handler** — `/preflight` takes the same model, and pricing the job is how somebody decides whether to commit to a target at all. Required on the model it would also turn the handler's sentence into a 422 field-path nobody can act on. | D-374 | 2026-09-09 | **The regulated 60-file / 120,000-character cap is now reported, not applied silently** — `main._regulated_files` returns `considered`/`assessed`/`capped`/`cap_note`, which travels into the prompt (`_regulated_context`), onto the free quote, into the run confirmation and back on the result as `coverage_limit`. | `attest.coverage_claim` **cannot** catch this one, and that is the whole point: it reconciles the model's claim against *the input it was given*, which was already the truncated 60. So a 400-file system had 60 files assessed, the report said nothing about the other 340, and every existing guard agreed with it — at exactly the point an FDA-facing report is least allowed to overstate its coverage. Adding a repository input is what made it urgent: a repo hits the cap far more easily than a hand-assembled zip. Stated four times over rather than once because each audience is different — the quote is where somebody decides to split the system, the confirmation is the last chance to stop, the prompt is what stops the model generalising to files it never saw, and `coverage_limit` is what a stored run can be re-read from. Both regulated zip doors were folded onto the same helper in the same pass, so a zip and a repository of the same code are capped, priced and described identically. **Measuring it found a second defect in the same loop:** the character budget was applied as `text[:remaining]`, so the last file that fit was cut in half and handed to the model with nothing saying so — and "no audit-trail write in this file" is then a *wrong* finding in an FDA-facing report whenever the write was in the discarded half. A file now goes in whole or not at all, which on this repo's own backend took the assessment from 3 files (one of them partial) to 11 whole ones, and files individually larger than the entire budget are **named** because splitting the system will never reach them. See C-88 for what is still not covered. |
| D-375 | 2026-09-09 | **Generated code can be pushed to a repository, through a writer that is deliberately narrower than `fix_apply`** — `services/code_delivery.py` + `POST /api/reviews/{id}/deliver-code`, for Codegen Scaffold and Migration Execute; and `resolve_base_branch` is now shared with `fix_apply`. | Five judgements. (1) **A new branch only.** Apply Fixes has `same_pr`, which commits onto a branch somebody may have checked out, and it may because a review had already read that branch. **Nothing has read a scaffold** unless `auto_review` was ticked, so the same door here would be a different risk wearing the same label. (2) **A branch that already exists is refused, not appended to** — re-running a generation is cheap and easy to do twice, and a second generation stacked on the first is not something a person can see before it happens. (3) **The review status goes in the pull request body**, verbatim, including "NOT reviewed": whoever merges generated code should not have to work out whether anything read it. (4) **`_DELIVERABLE_RUNS` is a deny-by-default list of review types**, not a check on the shape of `result` — a review type absent from it cannot be pushed at all. Verified live against a real repository by the new `verify_code_delivery.py` (20 checks, writes but calls no model) and `verify_fix_apply.py` re-run in full (26 checks) because the shared function is on its write path. |
| D-376 | 2026-09-09 | **Legacy Modernization's pull-request workflow reached a screen** — a delivery panel on the artifacts tab calling the long-existing `POST /api/v1/modernization/runs/{id}/github-pr`. | It was written when the build loop was, and no UI ever offered it, so the only way to reach it was curl. That is not a cosmetic gap: the pull request carries `.github/workflows/modernization-build.yml`, which compiles the branch on the customer's own runner and posts the compiler output back, where failing units return to `ready` and are re-converted. **Nothing on this platform compiles converted code** (D-05), so until that loop closes a type error in generated C# reaches the customer rather than the agent (C-42) — and the door to closing it was unreachable. Same class of finding as the modernization repo-URL gap: the capability shipped, the screen did not. The route's own `PUBLIC_BASE_URL` refusal is surfaced verbatim, because "a callback cannot be derived from this request" is the commonest failure and the sentence explains it. |
| X-08 | 2026-09-09 | **Corrected: the two ad-hoc regulated zip uploaders were replaced, not kept alongside the new cards.** `uploadCFR21Zip` and `uploadGxPPlanZip` are deleted. | Leaving them would have meant two doors per regulated screen again, which is exactly the shape that let the unredacted intake survive (D-372). | X-09 | 2026-09-09 | **Corrected: `CODE_SOURCE_CARDS` is keyed by *screen*, not by product id.** It was keyed by product, and `runDesignFromCode` ended a Sensitive Data scan by calling `loadAgentRuns('sensitive-data')` — but that screen's key is `sensitive`, so the lookup missed and the "Previous runs" list silently never refreshed. | Found while adding three more cards, which is the point: the bug was invisible with three rows and would have been invisible with six. Keyed by screen, `AGENT_SCREENS` stays the single place a screen declares its result container, renderer and history — and `ui_examine.py` §20 now fails on any card whose screen has no row, whose result box is missing, or whose renderer is not a function. |
| D-370 | 2026-09-09 | **Architecture and Security Review read a codebase by being sent a computed *description* of it, not the code** — `backend/services/code_brief.py`, one model call, no chunking. | Chunking is the obvious move and would have been wrong. `chunked_review` works because twenty lists of findings concatenate into one list of findings; `ARCHITECTURE_TOOL_SCHEMA` has a **single** `quality_scores` object and a single `architecture_type`, so twenty partial guesses have no merge rule and averaging them is not one. And a repository does not fit in one call at all (`MAX_AGENT_INPUT_CHARS`). The brief reuses the modernization pipeline's deterministic layer end to end — `analyzers.discover` → `graph.build` → `evidence` → `architecture.render_inventory`, the same sequence `scripts/audit_source_analysis.py` drives — so the expensive half costs nothing: measured, 3.3MB of this repo's own backend became a 31KB brief describing 155 files, 287 edges, 8 levels and 2 cycles. Four things found by running it before wiring it, all fixed: `render_inventory` prints `loc` and `graph.build` never sets it, so **every file reported 0 lines** and a 2,000-line god object looked identical to an empty `__init__.py`; the evidence layer's `entrypoint` actually means "nothing else imports this", which is not the same claim and is now labelled as what it is; and the table/datastore extractors are regex matches over text that returned `and`, `any`, `each` and `every` as table names and named MongoDB and Oracle in a SQLite application, so they are now under a heading that says they are matched rather than resolved. **The coverage statement is the load-bearing part**: real edges exist only for Python, PHP, Java/Kotlin/Scala, C#/VB.NET and Delphi/Pascal, a JavaScript repository degrades to an annotated file listing (C-24), and one sentence — `coverage_note`, computed from `UnitFacts.source` rather than an extension list that would drift — goes to both the prompt and the screen, on the free preflight, *before* the money. Guarded by `smoke_review_sources.py` §32. |
| D-371 | 2026-09-09 | **Sensitive Data does the opposite: it fans out over the real source.** `SensitiveDataAgent.scan(files=...)` → `chunked_review.run(verify_evidence=False)`. | The distinction is the whole of Phase 3 and it is a property of the *question*, not of the input. A design review asks one holistic thing; a sensitive-data scan asks a per-file thing, so findings concatenate — and a structural summary would be actively worse than useless here, because **a summary of a codebase cannot contain the hardcoded key that is the finding**. So it goes in `_CHUNKABLE_ROUTES` (or it reserves for one call and makes forty — C-77; measured: 1.98MB quoted 79 calls and made exactly 79), while the two design routes deliberately stay out. `verify_evidence` stays off for a stronger reason than elsewhere: this schema asks for an **obfuscated** sample, so a substring check would discard precisely the findings that followed instructions. No second `attest` call on that path — `chunked_review` already refuses a degenerate response and floors the gate. |
| D-372 | 2026-09-09 | **The regulated products' zip intake moved onto `source_bundle`, and Architecture's `compare` mode takes one labelled box per option.** | Two independent fixes with the same shape: a second implementation that had quietly drifted from the first. (1) `_extract_zip_files` was a bare `zipfile` walk — **no secret redaction, no path-traversal guard, no symlink guard, no zip-bomb cap** — so a customer uploading a codebase to a 21 CFR Part 11 verification had their hardcoded credentials sent to the model verbatim, while the identical upload to Code Review had them redacted first. Now `from_zip_bytes` with the narrower regulated extension set passed as an *argument* rather than a fork; the 60-file/120,000-char caps stay because `review_estimate.for_regulated_files` is calibrated on them. Two behaviour changes come with it, both improvements: `.env` and friends are skipped as credential files rather than uploaded, and secrets in kept files are redacted — neither costs a Part 11 assessment anything, since a `.env` holds secrets and not evidence of an audit trail. (2) `compare` sent an identical payload to `review` with one word changed, so several designs arrived as unseparated prose. Now N named boxes, fenced and labelled. **Deliberately not fixed**: per-option scoring, which the single `quality_scores` object makes impossible without a schema change — and a schema change changes what the model answers (D-344) with no architecture eval to measure it. The screen says so instead of implying a scorecard that cannot come back. |
| D-368 | 2026-09-08 | **A review records which earlier reviews it was built from** — `reviews.derived_from` (JSON array), bound by contextvar (`context.bind_derived_from`), read and *verified* in `save_review`, rendered as links when a run is reopened. (1) **By contextvar, not a parameter**, for exactly the reason `project_id` travels that way: `save_review` has 19 callers that know nothing about where their artifact came from. The ids are read off the request body in `legacy_guard`, which is already the one place on the browser path that sees a body before the handler — it reads it for the budget reservation — so this cost no new call sites. (2) **A claim is not a fact.** The ids come from the caller, so `_verified_parent_reviews` keeps only those that exist *and* share the project; a citation the reader gets a 404 for would be a dead link in an audit trail. Filtering down is the safe direction: it under-claims rather than asserting a link nothing supports (D-345's rule). (3) **Absent, not empty** — a review nobody chained stores NULL, and nothing is backfilled, because an existing row genuinely does not know its parent. (4) **The Release Gate's provenance is its `review_ids`**, so it needs no second field and the count-only gap closes with it — *but only when it gated on them*: pasted `findings` override `review_ids` in `workflows/release_gate.py` so a gate can never judge a set nobody saw, and recording the ignored ids would assert precisely the link that rule exists to prevent. (5) **`/api/review/full` binds it itself**, inside `_run`, because it is a 202 job and so is deliberately absent from `legacy_guard`'s product map, and its body runs on a thread that does not inherit the request context. Declared on the browser-facing schemas via `DerivedFromMixin` so a field that changes what gets stored is in `/docs` rather than an extra Pydantic happens to ignore — with the docstring saying no handler reads it and why. **Known gap:** `codegen-advisory`'s legacy route reuses the v1 model, so its `derived_from` works but is undocumented; the whole `/api/v1` surface is unbound and would need per-route work. | D-369 | 2026-09-08 | **The three in-page handoffs are armed by the *renderer*, not by the run function** (`renderCodegenAdvisory`, `renderMigrationAssessment`, `renderTestScenarios`), and **`applyMigrationAssessment` now carries the assessment instead of only pretending to**. | Both were real defects hiding inside C-36's summary. The variables were set in `runCodegenAdvisory` / `runTestScenarioGeneration`, but the buttons that read them are drawn by the *renderer* — which `openAgentRun` also calls. So reopening a stored codegen run from history drew "Use this stack for the scaffold" over a null variable and the button silently did nothing, and reopening a test-scenario run left "Use Last Test Scenario Result" insisting you run one first. Setting them where the result is drawn fixes both cases at once and removes the second place they could drift. The migration one was worse: `applyMigrationAssessment` guarded on `_lastMigrationAssessment` and then **never read it**, copying the assessment form's *own* target and source fields across, so `recommended_approach`, `breaking_changes` and `rollback_strategy` reached the migration call nowhere — a button labelled "Use this for Execution" that used none of it. `execute_migration` has accepted a `requirement` since it was written and the browser had never sent one, so carrying the guidance forward needed no backend change. | D-366 | 2026-09-08 | **An agent screen explains itself: the compliance chips are buttons onto a glossary (`products/frameworks.py`, `GET /api/v1/frameworks`), and an "About this agent" link serves `docs/agents/<id>.md` with the API-reference sections stripped (`products/agent_docs.py`).** | Four judgements, each of which could have gone the other way. (1) **Narrative sections only.** `## Request` / `## Response` / `## Example` / `## Authentication` document the `/api/v1` endpoint's field names, and a screen sends different ones — Code Review's documented sources are three while the screen offers six — so showing them tells the reader about an interface that is not in front of them. This is D-291's trap, which is already why `Product.sources` is not rendered. `omitted_sections` names what was dropped rather than quietly serving less, and one paragraph is **rescued** from the dropped `## Response`: the `Gate logic:` line, present in six documents, which is the single thing a person facing an Amber most wants. (2) **A `kind` per framework**, because HIPAA is a law, SOC 2 is an opinion a CPA firm issues, CWE is a catalogue and STRIDE is a whiteboard technique — and the strip rendered all four as the same badge, which implies a regulatory obligation where there is only a method. Looking up the registry token alone silently under-reports — the Part 11 panel would show 5 controls instead of 8, missing exactly the audit-trail ones. | D-367 | 2026-09-08 | **Landmine: `scripts/smoke_plan_picker.js` could not pass, for three independent reasons, and had presumably never passed.** All three fixed; it now reports 60 checks, exit 0. | It is one of the nine scripts CLAUDE.md calls the entire regression net, and it was worth nothing. (1) A stale assertion: it searched the catalogue for "sensitive" and asserted `full` was excluded — true when written, false ever since `full` gained its opt-in sensitive-data stage (D-26) and said so in its own `description`, which the filter matches on. **The filter was correct and the test was wrong**; the expected set is now derived from the payload so it cannot rot the same way. (3) The failure-path fetch stub had no `clone()`, and `apiFetch` reads a failed response twice, so the check that a failed add reports inline rather than as an alert failed for the harness's reason and not the page's. **The reason all three survived is the same**: the script needs a `products.json` that is not in the repository, so it is only ever run deliberately, and a script nobody can run accumulates failures that look like the product's.
