The browser was never affected; it uses `/api/review/cfr21-verification` in `main.py`. **Why nothing caught it is the durable part**: `smoke_governance.py` §11 asserts against that handler with `inspect.getsource`, which reads the function's *text* and never calls the route, so it passed for the handler's whole life while the route was dead — D-317's lesson in a new place. The guard is therefore behavioural, not textual: for every product whose registry `endpoint` starts `POST /api/v1/agents`, §22 asks the running app whether the path exists, whether the operation declares a body, whether its `operationId` starts with `_` (which is exactly what a decorator on a helper looks like), and whether an anonymous POST is refused with 401. Four questions, 25 endpoints, **zero hits today** — which is what makes a single hit worth believing. |
| D-314 | 2026-09-04 | **`GitHubService.create_branch_at_sha` exists because `create_branch`'s fourth argument is a branch NAME.** `fix_apply.apply` was passing `source["commit_sha"]` into it. | `create_branch` resolves that argument through `/git/ref/heads/<name>`, so a commit sha there requested a branch literally named `61f9b8f…` and GitHub answered 404 — which means Apply Fixes' `pr` and `branch` deliveries had **never once succeeded**. Found by running one real fix against one real pull request. No offline test could reach it: every fix test stops at `plan`, which writes nothing, and section 25 asserts which deliveries are *offered*, never that taking one works. `workflows/autofix_pr.py` passes a real branch name and has always worked, which is why `create_branch` keeps its signature and the sha case became its own method — one that rejects a branch name with a sentence naming the wrong argument, rather than letting GitHub answer 422 about a malformed ref. |
| D-315 | 2026-09-04 | **A fix pull request targets the branch the review read, not the review's label.** `fix_apply.apply` branches on `source_type`; an unknown base is resolved with `GitHubService.get_default_branch`, not assumed to be `main`. | `base_ref` was `label.split("@")[-1]` for every source. A repository's label is `owner/repo@ref`, so that worked. A **pull request's** label is its *title*, so the fix pull request was opened with `base="CB-104: AI visit summary endpoint"` and GitHub answered a bare 422 naming nothing. The right answer was already written down in `_available_outputs`' own description of the option — "a pull request against the branch it came from" — which is `head_branch`. The `main` fallback went at the same time: it is a guess that fails on any repository still using master or develop, and fails as another opaque 422. |
| D-316 | 2026-09-04 | **Landmine, and the most important thing the live run found: the fixer invents APIs, because it is shown one file and nothing else.** Not fixed — recorded, with the fix sketched below. | A real VAPT fix of `carebrief-demo` PR #1 correctly closed six severe vulnerabilities (hardcoded key, SQL injection, broken object-level authorization, LLM-output XSS, PHI in logs, prompt injection) and produced a file that **cannot be imported**: it wrote `from app.auth import get_current_user, User`, and that module exports `current_clinician` and `require_patient_access`. It also imported `bleach`, which is not in the project's `requirements.txt`. The correct helper's own docstring says "Use on every patient-scoped route" — in a file `autofix_agent._fix_one_file` was never shown, because it sends exactly one file per call. Neither guardrail can catch this: the secret scan looks for credentials, and the re-review asks whether the file still holds a vulnerability, not whether it imports. This is precisely what `verification_note` warns about ("nothing here compiles or runs your code") and it is worth more as a demonstrated fact than as a caveat. **The cheap improvement is to put the review's file inventory in the fix prompt** — the paths are already recorded in `review_source_files`, so the fixer could at least be told which modules exist; the fuller one is to hand it the signatures it may import. Both are cheaper than a build step, and neither makes the output verified code. |
| D-345 | 2026-09-07 | **An assessment may state what it was given and what the model claimed, and may never turn the first into the second** (`attest.coverage_claim`). | `cfr21_agent.verify` ended with `if not result["files_scanned"]: result["files_scanned"] = [every file we sent]`. That converts "the model did not tell us what it read" into "all 400 files were verified" inside an **FDA 21 CFR Part 11** report, and it is worse than reporting nothing: an auditor reading a list of 400 verified files has no way to tell the list was written by us rather than by the assessment, and the screen printed it as "Files scanned (400)". Replaced by a reconciliation that reports `files_supplied`, `files_claimed`, the unclaimed gap with a sample, and — the part that turned out to matter as much — `files_not_supplied`, paths the assessment named that were never sent, which is usually an invented filename and is a reason to distrust the findings against them. The empty case is left empty and the note says what that means. The Part 11 screen now says "the assessment did not name the files it examined; that is not the same as it having examined all of them" where it used to print our own list back. |
| D-344 | 2026-09-07 | **`backend/agents/attest.py` gives every single-call agent the two protections only `chunked_review` had — a degenerate response is an error, and the gate is a floor — and evidence verification is deliberately opt-in.** | The platform's own thesis was that the good pipeline reached 2 of 28 products. Half of that turned out to be the wrong diagnosis: only **four** products take a file list at all and two of those already chunk, so fanning out over chunks solves a problem the rest do not have. What they were all missing is cheaper and more important: a response with no findings and no summary over a non-trivial artifact was stored as a clean pass (`looks_degenerate` existed and only `chunked_review` called it), and a model's Green stood over its own Critical (`reconcile_gate`, likewise). Both now apply in the three shared methods of `base_agent.py`, which covers the requirement, prompt, compliance, security and architecture products in one edit, plus the three regulated ones wired directly (`cfr21_agent`, `gxp_document_agent`, `sensitive_data_agent` — the last needed its own edit for the reason in D-346). **The judgement is the opt-in**: applying `verify_evidence` everywhere was the obvious move and would have been a regression. Only `CODE_REVIEW_TOOL_SCHEMA` and `VAPT_TOOL_SCHEMA` demand a verbatim quote; `REVIEW_TOOL_SCHEMA` has no evidence field (`_code_review_schema`'s own docstring: "none of them review something with files and line numbers, so requiring evidence of them would be asking for a field they cannot fill"), and the Part 11 schema asks for "File path / function / code pattern… 'not applicable / no code available' if assessing at the process level only" — a description. A substring check over a description discards a correct finding for being phrased as instructed. So the flag is off by default, `evidence_verified` is left **absent** rather than set False where nothing was checked (False means "we checked and could not confirm", which is the same class of untruth as the back-filled file list), and `result["attested"]` records which protections actually ran so two assessments can be told apart. `gate_key`/`risk_key` exist because the Part 11 verifier and the GxP document check name their verdict `overall_gate_status` — `reconcile_gate` would not have seen it — and the floored value is written back under **both** names so a screen cannot disagree with the stored record. |
| D-348 | 2026-09-07 | **C-38 decided: an Amber gate still does not reach a human, and the governance report stops saying it did.** One shared predicate, `finding_gate.requires_human_signoff(gate_status, critical_count)`, read by both `database.save_review` and `ReportGeneratorAgent._build_report_document`. | The report re-derived its own answer as `gate_status in ("Amber", "Red")` while the queue enqueues on Red-or-Critical, so **every Amber report told its reader a sign-off had been required when nobody was ever asked for one** — an assertion about a control that did not run, inside the document that exists to evidence that controls did run. Same class of defect as back-filling a compliance file list (D-345), and found the same way. The policy choice was the user's: extending the queue to Amber would raise approval volume for every existing customer on the next deploy and Amber is common, so the cheaper and more defensible fix was to stop the false claim rather than manufacture the control. Two copies of a rule is how they came to disagree, so there is now one function and a guard asserting both callers read it rather than spelling it out again. Also added `approval_basis`, because the old UI rendered **nothing** when no sign-off was required — so "never asked" and "asked and granted" looked identical on the page, which is what let a reader infer the control had run. If Amber is ever extended to a human, that function is the only place to change. |
| D-349 | 2026-09-07 | **`UI_PROJECT_MODE` replaces the `UI_REQUIRE_PROJECT` boolean with three states — `off` / `warn` / `enforce`, defaulting to `warn` — and the warning reaches the browser as a response header, not a body key.** | The boolean could only offer "anything goes" or "refused", and flipping it refuses every run from a session that has not picked a project, which is why C-80 kept being deferred rather than decided. The middle state is what makes enforcement shippable. Three things worth knowing before touching it: (1) `UI_REQUIRE_PROJECT` is now **derived** (`mode == "enforce"`) and kept only so existing readers and `UI_REQUIRE_PROJECT=true` deployments are unchanged — an explicit `UI_PROJECT_MODE` wins, and an unrecognised value falls back to `warn` rather than `off`, because a typo is not evidence that governance was meant to be disabled. (2) The resolution is a **function**, `config.resolve_project_mode`, because `smoke_products.py` §14 asserts shipped defaults by reading `config.py` as *source* (a reload picks up the developer's own `.env` and would report a bypass closed while it is open) and a derived value cannot be read that way. (3) **The legacy path was the whole job.** `guarded_call` can attach `_project_warning` to the body, but the browser uses the synchronous `/api/review/*` routes, which `legacy_guard` meters *around* the handler — so it goes out as `X-Project-Warning`, named in the CORS `expose_headers`, and `apiFetch` folds it in with the body winning if both are present. It is set **above** `legacy_guard`'s `if not usage["calls"]: return` on purpose: whether a run was governed has nothing to do with whether a model was reached, and a free preflight is exactly when a person still has time to pick a project. |
| D-350 | 2026-09-08 | **C-37 decided and built: an approval is one decision per *release*, and a release is one API call.** `context.current_release_id` is minted in `bind_project`; `save_review` reuses the release's pending approval instead of raising a second, and `approval_reviews` records what the decision covers. | A Core SDLC Review calls `save_review` three times — code review, VAPT, aggregate — and each satisfied `requires_human_signoff`, so one run put **three** items in a reviewer's queue that were all the same question. The user's call, with the note that pending rows do not need migrating because nothing is in production yet. Four things worth knowing. (1) **The release is the API call**, because both guards already open `bind_project` exactly once per call — so this cost no new call sites and no threading through the 19 callers of `save_review`, which is the same reasoning that put `project_id` in `context` to begin with. (2) **Nesting inherits**: `bind_project` sets the id only `or`-wise, or an inner block would split one run back into the rows this merges. (3) **Priority escalates and never relaxes** — a release holding one Red is urgent even when the run that raised the request was Amber — and `list_approvals` reports `release_gate` as the *strictest* across covered runs for the same reason, so an Amber row can never stand for a Red release. (4) **Outside a request there is no release** and each review raises its own, which is the honest fallback: a smoke script or a `scripts/` driver has nothing grouping its reviews, and pretending otherwise would merge unrelated ones. `get_pending_approval_for_review` now matches through the link table so any member can still report that it is waiting on a human (`workflows.shared.attach_approval` asks exactly that, per review). C-39 closed with it: the card names the covered runs and links each to its own findings via `_screenForReviewType` — note `full` **is** in `AGENT_SCREENS` and does link; modernization and the governance report are the ones deliberately absent. |
| D-351 | 2026-09-08 | **An artifact too large for one call now says how many runs' worth it is, and that nothing was spent** (`guardrail_service.enforce_input_limit`). Message only — the limit and the refusal are unchanged. | "Split the artifact into smaller pieces" was true and unactionable: it did not say whether you were 5% over or four times over, so the only way to find out was to resubmit repeatedly. The size is the one thing that function knows for certain. Two details that matter more than they look: it is **ceiling** division, because 1.01x the limit still needs a second run; and it states that nothing was spent, because `enforce_input_limit` runs at the top of `call_tool` before any token is used and a refusal on a big codebase otherwise reads as a charge for nothing. **This is not the fix for the real gap** — the regulated products still cannot produce one report over a whole system, cannot accept a zip or a repository at all (`cfr21-verification` takes `files`, `code`, `github_pr_url`, `github_file_url`), and have no `/preflight`, so there is no cost quote before the button. `_preflight_quote` is already agent-parameterised and would generalise cheaply; the batching and the merge rule behind it are the week of work. |
| D-352 | 2026-09-08 | **A project's own spend is readable by an admin of that project, on separate routes whose scope is the URL** — `/api/v1/usage/projects/{project_id}/{summary,events,events.csv}`. | The ledger was super-admin only, so the person who owned a budget could see the cap enforced and never the spend against it: "why was I refused?" had no answer they could look up. Three deliberate choices. (1) **Separate routes, not a relaxed dependency** on the existing three — the cross-customer view is the privileged thing and a shared handler is one `if` away from leaking it. (2) **`_scoped` overrides the `project_id` query filter with the path segment**, which is the security property and not a convenience: `_filters` accepts that parameter for the super-admin routes, so without the override a project admin could pass another id. `_project_filters` omits it entirely, so there is no parameter to try. A test asserts the path wins. (3) **Project admins, not members** — the membership/admin split exists so a developer can work in a project without being handed its API keys, and its spend is the same category. 404 on an unknown project, matching the read-scoping discipline. **Landmine:** the frontend's `isProjectAdmin()` tests `_authUser.role === 'project_admin'` while the server tests a `project_admins` row — two different notions, and the Projects nav already had this. A user with the role but no row now gets an explanatory empty state rather than a 403; fixing the mismatch properly means deciding whether the role column or the table is the truth. |
| D-353 | 2026-09-08 | **The audit trail records the events an auditor asks about, and can be exported.** New: failed and rejected sign-ins, user created/deactivated, GitHub credential created/updated/deleted, a fix delivered or exported, retained source deleted. `database.audit_event` is the public entry point; `GET /api/audit/export.csv` is the file. | The trail recorded reviews, approvals, successful logins and notification targets — and none of the write paths. A failed sign-in went to stdout and nowhere else, so the one surface that exists to answer "who did what" could not show an attempt, let alone a run of them. Four things worth knowing. (1) **`fix_apply` records at the top of `_deliver`, before the write** — that is the single funnel both `apply` and `deliver` pass through, it has several exits, and for an audit trail an *attempted* write to a customer repository is itself the event; a failure still reaches the caller as an exception. `diff` and `zip` are recorded as `fix_exported` because taking a patch out of the platform is also a governance event. (2) **Never a secret in `details`** — these rows are admin-readable and exported as CSV, so a credential row says `token_replaced=1` and a key row says its scopes and cap, never the value; tests assert the absence. (3) **Login events are deployment-wide (`project_id=""`)**, so one customer's admin cannot read another's sign-in attempts; the export uses the identical `scope.visible_from_caller` as the page, so it can never contain a row the page would hide. (4) **Landmine found while doing it:** `api_key_created` and `api_key_revoked` *already* existed inside `database`, so adding route-level rows double-logged every key. The rows were improved instead — a project id (the route opens no `bind_project`, so they were landing deployment-wide and invisible to the project's own admin) and a real actor for revocation, which recorded `"admin"` unconditionally, the same defect `review_created` had with `'system'`. Grep for an existing event before adding one. |
| D-354 | 2026-09-08 | **A 21 CFR Part 11 verification of a system too large for one call is assessed in parts rather than refused, and priced first.** One call while the prompt fits; otherwise `chunked_review.run` with `verify_evidence=False` and a `merge_extra` that takes the strictest reading of the four §11 assessments. | The product could not produce a report for the size of application that buys it, and the refusal arrived only after the button was pressed. Five things to know. (1) **A system that fits is unchanged** — same prompt, same single call, no new cost on the common path; only `fits = len(prompt) <= MAX_AGENT_INPUT_CHARS` decides. (2) **`verify_evidence` had to become a parameter** of `finding_pipeline.process` / `process_files` and of `chunked_review.run`: this schema asks for "File path / function / code pattern" and accepts "not applicable / no code available", so the substring check would discard correct findings for being phrased as instructed (D-344). Everything else in the pipeline still runs. (3) **`_merge_raw` was silently dropping every non-prose field** — it joined strings and ignored the rest, which was invisible while only Code Review and VAPT chunked because neither has one, and would have emptied four of the six things an FDA report is made of. Structured fields are now gathered and handed to `merge_extra`; without one the first is kept and `partial_fields` says so, because a clumsy merge beats a silent hole. (4) **The merge rule is the compliance judgement and it lives with the agent**: applicable if *any* part found it applicable (a part with no signature code says "not applicable" truthfully about itself and must not erase another part's finding), compliant only if *every* applicable part said so, gaps unioned, each part's prose kept under its own heading rather than re-summarised — a summarising call costs more and can assert what no part established. (5) **The quote needed its own entries** in `review_estimate.MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS` and `_frame_tokens`, or it priced a 16k-output agent at the 8k default against the *code reviewer's* prompt — the same defect the `auto-fix` branch there already documents. A zip preflight exists as well as the JSON one because the browser uploads a zip. **Correction to earlier analysis:** the claim that these products "cannot accept a whole system" was wrong — `POST /api/review/cfr21-verification/upload-zip` has existed all along; what was missing was the chunking and the quote. |
| D-355 | 2026-09-08 | **The Dashboard gains the two things six lifetime totals cannot say: which repository is in trouble, and whether this is getting better.** `GET /api/dashboard/trend` serves `get_findings_trend` and `get_gate_trend`; the repository grid calls the `GET /api/repositories` that already existed. | Four things worth knowing. (1) **`/api/repositories` had been serving nobody** — it shipped with the repository columns and no browser code ever called it, so the answer to "how is each of our repositories doing" was computed and unreachable. The grid is a renderer, not a subsystem. (2) **Findings have no date of their own** — they inherit the review's `created_at` through the join, so "new this week" means "reported by a run this week", which is the honest reading: a finding did not exist for us before the run that found it. `resolved` reads `resolved_at`. (3) **`open_at_end` is a running total computed in Python**, not a correlated subquery per week — on SQLite that is one scan instead of `weeks` of them — and it is clamped at zero, because a finding resolved in a week whose review predates the window would otherwise drive it negative. (4) **Findings and gates are separate series on purpose**: one Red release can carry forty findings and forty Green ones carry none, so averaging them would answer neither question. Drawn with CSS width bars, matching every other bar-like widget here — **choosing a charting library is a real decision** with a bundle cost in a project with no build step, and should not be made as a side effect of adding one panel. |
| D-356 | 2026-09-08 | **A command palette (Ctrl/Cmd-K) that reads the sidebar rather than keeping its own list of screens.** | 37 nav items grouped by the *builder's* taxonomy — "Requirement Intelligence", "Developer Intelligence" — so reaching a screen meant remembering which module its author filed it under, and the whole application had exactly one keyboard shortcut (Escape). **Reading the DOM is the load-bearing choice:** a table of screens would be a second place to register every new one and would be wrong the first time somebody forgot — the drift `AGENT_SCREENS` already needs a smoke check to catch. It also means a nav item hidden by role is absent from the palette for free, instead of offering somebody a screen the server will refuse. Matching is a subsequence, so "gxpdoc" finds "GxP Document Check", and the group name is searchable because for some screens it is the only label a person knows. Opening one goes through `_setHash`, so a palette jump is a real address and Back works — a silent page swap would have undone D-?'s deep links. Bound to Ctrl/Cmd-K and not a bare key, or it would fire while somebody is typing a requirement into a textarea. |
| D-357 | 2026-09-08 | **The GxP document check is deliberately NOT chunked, and that is the end of "generalise the review substrate" as an honest goal.** | The platform review said the good pipeline reached 2 of 28 products and implied 26 were waiting. D-344 already corrected half of that: only **four** products take a file list at all. Code Review and VAPT chunked; `cfr21-verification` now does (D-354); the fourth is the document check, and splitting it would be **wrong rather than merely unnecessary**. It scores a document against a required-elements checklist, so a part that does not contain the revision history reports it missing — every part would report almost everything missing, and "missing only if absent from all parts" makes `completeness_score` mean nothing. Documents are also not codebases; an SOP does not reach 400,000 characters. So it keeps one call and the improved refusal (D-351). Everything else has `attest` (D-344/D-346), which is the part of the pipeline that was universal. **Do not reopen this as a coverage gap** — the remaining products either have no findings and no gate, or take a single pasted artifact where chunking solves a problem they do not have. |
| D-358 | 2026-09-08 | **The eval was re-run after 22 commits and passed, but the run revealed that its gate floor is a coin flip: three runs of one 10-line case at one prompt version gave Amber, Amber, Green with 0% finding overlap.** Accuracy did not regress; `MIN_GATE_ACCURACY` is measuring noise. | Re-measured because `finding_pipeline.py`, `finding_gate.py`, `code_chunker.py` and `chunked_review.py` all changed after D-245, and those four decide which findings survive and what gate comes out - so the floors were guarding a pipeline that no longer shipped. Result: **recall 100% (unchanged), false positives 12.5% -> 0% of 8 traps, 0 hallucinated, $0.19**; gate accuracy 100% -> 90%, which is exactly the floor. The drop is not a regression. `fp-framework-csrf` is the only case whose expected gate is sensitive to a judgement call, and `--repeat 3` on it returned Amber/Amber/Green with **0% finding overlap across runs** - the baseline's Green was one sample, not a property, and that same case tripped the CSRF trap in the run that set the floors. The mechanism is exact: every run reports the *same* correct finding (`request.POST["bio"]` raises `KeyError` -> HTTP 500), and the gate turns on whether the model rates a generic "unvalidated input" concern severity 3 or 2. So D-245's "floors one step below each measurement" is unsound on a 10-case corpus, where one step below 100% is one case: the tripwire fires on variance rather than on a change. **The floors were deliberately not lowered** - lowering a floor to accommodate noise removes the only regression detector this project has. C-85 holds the fix. |
| D-359 | 2026-09-08 | **The eval's gate floor is applied only to cases that gave the same verdict in every run, and `--repeat` defaults to 3.** An inconsistent case is named and counted against `MAX_UNSTABLE_GATE_CASES` instead of being averaged into the score; `score()` and `thresholds()` are pure and driven offline by `smoke_code_review.py` §16. | Closes C-85. A case that answers Amber, Amber, Green cannot evidence a pass *or* a regression, and pooling it did both kinds of harm: the 2026-09-08 full run read exactly 90.0% (the floor) with nothing wrong, and a two-case verification run read **66.7% pooled against 100% over the one case that was consistent** - a number that would have been reported as a catastrophic regression. Rejected: lowering the floor (it is the only regression detector this project has, and the script still printed "OK" so nobody would have noticed), and relaxing that case to accept Green-or-Amber (deletes the assertion rather than repairing it). Three details decided the shape. (1) Stability is keyed on **correctness, not the gate letter** - a case wrong three different ways is stably broken, and keying on the letter would let a case that is never right hide in the excluded set. (2) **Recall and traps stay pooled**, because they were stable in all five measured runs; majority-voting recall would round a real miss away to "the case passed". (3) A **growing** unstable set is itself a failure, because falling reproducibility shows up in no accuracy number - the ceiling is 1, measured, so a second coin flip fails the run. `--repeat 1` still reduces exactly to the old pooled arithmetic and now prints what it cannot answer. `gate_accuracy` therefore means something different from result files written before this, so every file records `scoring: stable-cases-v2` and keeps `gate_accuracy_pooled` beside it. Instability reproduced a fifth time during verification, in a different order again (Green/Amber/Amber). |
| D-360 | 2026-09-08 | **The 10 upload handlers run their model call *and* their PDF/zip parsing through `asyncio.to_thread`, and the structural guard now tests the property that would have caught it.** | Closes C-83. **Measured, per C-31's own instruction: `GET /` blocked 5.30s of a 6s review before, 0.28s after** - the same shape as D-334's 5.45s -> 0.30s. Three things are worth keeping. (1) **These routes cannot drop `async`** like the 23 in D-334, because they must `await file.read()`; that is also why `smoke_products.py` §19 passed them for a year - its rule is "async and never awaits", and these await. The new rule is exact: inside an `async def` route an agent call must be *passed to* `to_thread`, never *called*, with nested function bodies excluded so the three 202 job routes still pass. (2) **The parsing blocked too, and before the model was reached** - `_extract_text` walks a PDF page by page under PyPDF2 and `_extract_zip_files` decompresses to the CFR21 char cap, so a 300-page upload froze the server even on a request that was about to be refused. Six text extractions and three zip extractions moved, not just the eight model calls. (3) **`to_thread` hands the call a *copy* of the context**, so cost attribution could have silently stopped: `legacy_guard` learns which review to bill from `context.collect_saved_reviews()`, which reports *back up*. It survives only because D-311 made it a mutable list - the thread appends to the object the middleware still holds - and that is now asserted rather than reasoned about, because the same code with a plain value would have unbilled every upload with nothing failing. `upload_kb_doc` is deliberately untouched: one small `.md` file, no model call. |
| D-361 | 2026-09-08 | **A live waiver outranks every other row for the same fingerprint, and `get_finding_baseline` picks its row with `ROW_NUMBER()` instead of bare columns.** Ties break on the most recent decision, then `f.id`. | Closes C-81, and the defect was worse than that row described. It is not "whichever row SQLite picked": the query had exactly one `MIN(r.created_at)`, which triggers SQLite's documented min/max special case, so the bare columns came from the row with the **minimum** - the *oldest* review. Verified empirically on 3.45.3 rather than reasoned about. That makes the broken case the **normal waiver lifecycle**: review 1 sees a finding open, someone waives it from review 2's list, and every run after that reads the oldest row and reports it open again. A team's explicit acceptance was invisible for ever, and the status and its attribution could come from different rows (the oldest row has no resolver). **The rule is "any live waiver wins", not "newest row wins"**, because the two fail differently and asymmetrically: a stale waiver hides one finding and is already bounded - `accepted_risk` expires after 90 days and `lapse_expired_waivers` runs before every baseline lookup, which is why `waiver_lapsed_at IS NULL` is in the predicate even though a lapsed row is also set back to `open` (belt and braces, D-259) - whereas re-reporting a decided finding devalues every review after it. Both SQL strings in the function were changed; fixing one and not the other is the obvious half-fix, so the project-less branch has its own check. Swept for the same pattern elsewhere: `get_review_spend` aggregates explicitly with `MAX()`, and `list_repositories` already avoids it with a second query (`database.py:1327`), so this was the only instance. |
| D-362 | 2026-09-08 | **`scripts/verify_fix_apply.py` drives all five Apply Fixes deliveries plus the preview against a real repository, and `smoke_review_sources.py` §31 fails if `fix_apply.OUTPUTS` gains a delivery it has no stage for.** | The counterpart D-317 said was missing. Fix delivery is the one subsystem here where defects have demonstrably hidden, and they hid structurally rather than by luck: every offline fix test stops at `plan`, which writes nothing, so D-314 (a commit sha passed where a branch *name* is resolved - the `branch` and `pr` deliveries had never once succeeded), D-315 (a pull request's label is its *title*, so the base branch was 'CB-104: AI visit summary endpoint') and D-325 (`UnboundLocalError` in the fallback written to turn a 422 into a sentence) all lived in the same twenty lines and were all found by reading. Each of those three now has a check that only a real write can satisfy: the branch exists on GitHub and was cut at the commit the review read, and the pull request's base is a branch that actually resolves. Two judgements. **`same_pr` is excluded from the default stages** because it commits onto a branch somebody else may have checked out - it is the one delivery that changes an existing pull request rather than adding something beside it. And the offline guard checks **coverage of `OUTPUTS`, not behaviour**, because behaviour is exactly what cannot be checked offline; a sixth delivery with no stage would put this subsystem back to no coverage at all. Verified only as far as it can be without a scratch repository: every API it calls resolves, `--help` renders, and the five stage names match `OUTPUTS` - **no live run has happened**, and until one does D-317's landmine still stands. |
| D-363 | 2026-09-08 | **`smoke_products.py` §21: no function may be called with more positional arguments than it accepts, anywhere under `backend/`.** Bare-name calls to module-level definitions in the same file; `*args` splats skipped. | The general form of the first defect in D-364, which was a guaranteed `TypeError` on an unconditional line that survived eight commits. Worth having because it reports **zero** across `backend/` today, so a single hit is real rather than noise — and worth contrasting with the "does every bare name resolve" sweep tried earlier in this programme and abandoned: that one found 87 call sites against 516 declarations and would have given false confidence instead of coverage. Deliberately does not attempt keyword-only mismatches or cross-module calls; a check that claims less and is trusted beats one that claims more and is muted. Reverting the fix fails it with both line numbers named. |
| D-364 | 2026-09-08 | **Apply Fixes was broken in three separate places, all found by `verify_fix_apply.py`'s first real run against a scratch repository, and all now fixed and re-verified: 26 checks, 0 failures, a real branch and a real pull request.** | D-317's landmine is finally closed, and it cost exactly what that row predicted. **(1) `_available_outputs(source, loaded["review"])` against a one-argument definition** (`fix_apply.py:540` and `:570`) — a `TypeError` on an unconditional line, so *every* call to `apply` and `deliver` raised before reaching any delivery. Apply Fixes had been **entirely non-functional since `0b2ca6b`**, the commit that introduced the preview, eight commits earlier. Guarded now by D-363. **(2) A GitHub zipball names its root directory `{owner}-{repo}-{short_sha}` with only 7 characters**, and `POST /git/refs` rejects an abbreviated sha with a bare 422 — so `branch` and `pr` were impossible for every `github_repo` source, while a `github_pr` source was fine because it takes `head_sha` from the API. Fixed with `GitHubService.expand_commit_sha`, called from `from_github_repo`: it names the *same* commit in full rather than re-resolving the branch, so the reason `sha_from_zipball` reads the archive at all (a busy branch must not move underneath us) still holds. Expanded at intake rather than at the write, because a 7-character sha on an audit record is ambiguous by construction. **(3) `label.split("@")[-1]` returned the repository name** for a repository reviewed at its default branch, whose label carries no `@` — so `POST /pulls` got a base branch called `scratch-repo` and answered 422, and the `if not base_ref` fallback could not see it because the value was not empty. This is D-315's family: a branch name derived from a human label. Now the split only happens when an `@` is present, **and** the result is checked with the new `GitHubService.branch_exists` before use — which also catches a ref that is a tag, a sha, or a branch since deleted, all of which fail as the same opaque 422. The fix the model produced was correct on inspection: both injections parameterized, the `LIKE` wildcard moved into the bound parameter, the secret moved to `os.environ`, no invented helper (contrast D-316). |
| D-346 | 2026-09-07 | **Landmine: editing a shared method in `base_agent.py` does not reach a subclass that overrides `_call_claude`.** `SensitiveDataAgent` overrides it and `scan` calls it directly, so it never went through `BaseAgent.review` and got none of D-344's protections — attested explicitly instead (`sensitive_data_agent.py`). | D-344 asserted the base-class edit covered sensitive-data; it did not, and nothing failed, because a missing guard is invisible until the response it would have refused actually arrives. Checked the other three direct-gateway agents at the same time: `ai_provenance_agent`, `issue_triage_agent` and `migration_agent` return no `findings` and no gate, so `attest` is a genuine no-op for them and they are deliberately left alone — there is no verdict to floor and inventing one would judge work nobody asked to have judged. **Sensitive-data is also the strongest case for `verify_evidence` staying off**: its schema asks for `sample` to be *obfuscated* with the real data replaced by `[REDACTED]`, so a substring check against the source would fail for every correctly-masked finding — verification would discard exactly the findings that followed instructions. `smoke_code_review.py` section 14 now drives all three regulated agents rather than only unit-testing `attest`, because that is the failure mode: the module can be correct and fully tested and still reach only the agents somebody remembered. |
| D-347 | 2026-09-07 | **Landmine: the two regulated `/api/v1` responses are hand-written allowlists, not `_wrap`, so a new result field reaches the browser and not the API.** `coverage_claim`, `attested` and `model_gate_status` had to be named explicitly in the `cfr21-verification` and `gxp-document-check` responses (`v1_agents.py`). | `_wrap` ends with a `k not in CORE` spread, so every other agent carries unknown keys out for free — these two build their JSON by hand and silently drop anything not listed. It bit exactly the wrong pair: since `files_scanned` is no longer back-filled (D-345), an integrator who sees an empty list needs `coverage_claim` to tell them it means "the assessment did not say" rather than "nothing was scanned" — without it the honest answer looks like a bug and invites someone to re-add the back-fill. The UI was never affected: `renderReviewTransparency` reads the stored `result_json`, not the API response, which is why this was invisible on screen. |
| D-343 | 2026-09-07 | **GitHub App support (`backend/services/github_app.py`) and a check run with per-line annotations, created *in addition to* the commit status.** The JWT is signed with `cryptography`, not PyJWT. | A check run is the only thing that puts a finding on the line it is about, and GitHub refuses `POST /check-runs` for a personal access token whatever its scopes — so this needed an App or it was impossible; `--sarif` (D-335) is the cheap route to roughly the same place and has none of the placement. Decisions worth knowing. **Additive, never a replacement:** a team's branch protection may already require the commit status by name, and swapping it for a differently-named check would stop protecting the branch while appearing to work — so both are sent, one extra request. **Amber concludes `neutral`, not `action_required`:** whether an Amber gate should block a merge is the open policy question C-38 records, and choosing here would answer it silently for every customer. **An unplaceable finding is not placed:** no file, no line, or a quote not found in the file it names means the line number is a guess, and an annotation on the wrong line sends a reviewer to code that is fine and teaches them to distrust the tool — those stay in the summary, counted. **Signed with `cryptography`** because PyJWT is importable here but is not in `requirements.txt` (it arrives through litellm), and an authentication path resting on an undeclared dependency disappears at the next upgrade; `cryptography` is declared, already used by `crypto_service`, and this is fifteen lines. Installation tokens are cached in memory only, dropped five minutes early (a token that expires mid-request fails a write already paid for) and never written to SQLite, where a bearer credential with write access to a customer's repository would outlive the process that needed it. **Not verified: the two network calls have never reached GitHub.** Everything offline-checkable is (`smoke_review_sources.py` §34: the JWS structure, the claims, a signature that verifies against its own key, the flattened-PEM case, the annotation and conclusion mappings, the 50 cap, the unplaceable count, the 403/404 hints); `verify_pr_review.py` now covers the check run and is the only thing that can prove it. `docs/github-app.md` says all of this to the person setting it up. |
| D-342 | 2026-09-07 | **A pull-request review now publishes its source, so `reviews.repo_owner`/`repo_name`/`pr_number`/`commit_sha` are populated for the path where the repository is least ambiguous.** | Pre-existing gap, found while building D-341 and load-bearing for it. D-327 added the repository dimension and `save_review` reads it from `context.current_source_ref()`, but only three call sites ever published one (`main.py`, and two in `v1_agents.py`) — `workflows/review_pr.py` published none, so **every pull-request review ever stored had NULL in all six columns**. `GET /api/repositories`, the findings `repo` filter and `previously_open_findings` were therefore blind to pull requests, and D-341's carry-forward lookup (which keys on `pr_number`) would have returned nothing every time, silently, producing exactly the wrongly-Green gate it exists to prevent. Fixed by building the `Bundle` once at the top of the workflow and binding `bundle.source_ref()` across both the review and the save — which also gets the repository's `.lmignore` to a pull-request review (D-337) and stops the source inventory building a second bundle and fetching the ignore file twice. |
| D-341 | 2026-09-07 | **`synchronize` reviews only the commits pushed since the last review (`INCREMENTAL_PR_REVIEW`, default on), and still-open findings from earlier reviews of the same pull request are carried into the result and still vote in the gate.** | `synchronize` fires on every push, so an active 40-file pull request cost a full review per push — the number that gets an integration switched off rather than fixed. The saving is easy and the danger is the whole of the work: a run that reads only the newest commits does not see a Critical raised two pushes ago in a file it never opened, **and an absent finding reads as a fixed one** — so the stored gate goes Green and `set_commit_status` clears the merge button. `review_pr._carry_forward` closes that: `database.open_findings_for_pr` (keyed on `pr_number`, not on the repository — another open pull request's findings are not this one's problem) returns still-open findings, they are appended to `findings` labelled `carried_forward` with `evidence_verified: None` (it was verified against the file *then*; this run never opened the file), and `reconcile_gate` runs again over the combined list. Three deliberate non-choices. **`coverage_incomplete` is not set** for the narrowing — it exists for code nobody chose to skip, and clamping every incremental review to Amber would mean a clean push could never be Green, which is the point; the scope is carried by the findings and by a line in the comment instead. **Carried findings go in `findings`, not a second list** — the gate, the counts, the stored rows and the comment all read one list. **The model's `model_gate_status` is preserved across the second `reconcile_gate`**, which otherwise reads the already-floored value as the model's own answer and destroys the disagreement rate the suite measures. Fallbacks are wide on purpose: a force-push or rebase (GitHub reports `diverged`/`behind`, so the old sha is no longer an ancestor and `old...new` would both miss code and mislabel the rest), a compare that cannot be fetched, an empty slice, or no earlier review — all review the whole pull request and say why on the result. The trade, stated in `config.py`: files changed two pushes ago are not re-read, so a problem that only appears in the interaction between old and new code waits for the next full review. |
| D-340 | 2026-09-07 | **Channels are outbound webhooks only — Slack, Teams and a generic receiver from one implementation — and a target URL is treated as a credential and screened for SSRF.** | One decision, three consequences. **Webhook only:** Slack, Teams and anything else all accept an incoming-webhook URL, so one sender covers all three with a per-destination body shape (`notify.shape`), no OAuth app and no credential beyond the URL. Email is deliberately absent: it needs SMTP this deployment has none of, and a configured-looking channel that silently sends nothing is worse than no channel. **The URL is a credential:** possession of a Slack webhook URL is authority to post to that channel, so it is encrypted at rest and never returned by any endpoint — `database._redact_target` gives a host and four characters, which distinguishes two channels and cannot be used as one; same rule as `llm_connections`. It is also not editable (rename or delete, never repoint), because a channel whose destination changed while its name and delivery history stayed is a log that means something other than what it says. **SSRF:** a user-supplied URL that *this server* requests would otherwise make the platform a proxy into its own network, so `https` is required, the host is resolved and every returned address checked against loopback/link-local/private/multicast space (link-local is where cloud metadata lives), the check runs again immediately before each send rather than being trusted from the row, and redirects are not followed — a 302 to a link-local address would walk straight past all of it. Not closed: DNS rebinding between the check and the connect, which needs a transport pinning the validated address; stated in the module docstring rather than left unsaid. |
| D-339 | 2026-09-07 | **Notifications: the delivery is a durable row written inside `save_review`'s own transaction, attempted on a daemon thread, claimed atomically, and retried at startup and on the delivery-log read** (`backend/services/notify.py`). | Every result on this platform was a pull — somebody logs in, or CI reads an exit code — so a Red gate at 6pm was found at 9am. With no scheduler and no queue there were three options: fire inline in the review handler (**rejected outright** — it puts a third-party HTTP call inside a request, which is exactly the defect D-334 measured and fixed), fire on a thread and forget (drops silently on restart, with no way to find out), or build a job runner (the honest answer, and far more change than notifications justify). This is the second made recoverable: `notify.enqueue_for_review` writes on **the caller's cursor**, so a notification cannot describe a review that failed to save and a saved review cannot lose its notification; `kick()` fires a daemon thread *after* the commit, because the dispatcher opens its own connection and would not see an uncommitted row. `_claim` is the concurrency guarantee — one `UPDATE ... WHERE status='pending'` decides the winner, so two uvicorn workers, or a kicked thread and a read-path retry, divide the work instead of double-posting; found by a test where a row was attempted twice, which `kick()`'s in-process lock could never have prevented. A row in flight is `sending` and `recover_sending()` returns it to `pending` at startup, the same reasoning as `recover_orphaned_jobs`. `review_gated` fires on exactly the condition that already enqueues an approval, so a channel and the approvals queue cannot disagree about whether something was blocked. What this does **not** buy: promptness after a crash — a row written seconds before a restart is late, visible and not lost, and if that is not good enough the answer is the job runner. |
| D-338 | 2026-09-07 | **The migration path off click-by-click waivers is an export, not an import: `GET /api/v1/findings/lmignore` (and `lmsdlc findings --lmignore`) renders a project's waivers as a file to review.** | A team that has been waiving the same findings for months has those decisions in this database only — invisible in code review, gone on a fork, and re-entered from zero on the next project. The export is what makes suppression as code worth adopting rather than a second place to say the same thing. It hands over a **file to read** instead of writing into the repository, because a rule is necessarily **wider** than the waiver it came from: a waiver is keyed on a fingerprint (control + path + title + evidence) and a rule can only express the path and the control, so an exported rule also covers a *different* finding of the same control in the same file. The file's own header says so. Two things deliberately do not survive: an **expiry**, because a file has no clock — `database.list_waived_findings` filters on `waiver_lapsed_at IS NULL` so a lapsed acceptance is left out rather than exported as permanent, which is the one thing the expiry exists to prevent; and a **waiver with no file path** (a design finding), which is emitted as a `# SKIPPED` comment rather than dropped, because a waiver that vanishes on export is one somebody still has to re-enter by hand. |
| D-337 | 2026-09-07 | **Suppression as code: `.lmignore` at the repository root and inline `lm-ignore` comments (`backend/services/suppression.py`). An ignore rule lowers the gate and never hides the evidence.** | A waiver was a row in this database set by a person clicking — right for a decision somebody signs, wrong for the much larger class of "this is a fixture", "this tree is deleted in Q3", "this line is deliberate and we have said so four times". Those were re-entered per project, invisible in code review, and did not travel to a fork. Four things about the design are load-bearing. **(1) Suppressed, not deleted** — a matched finding moves to `suppressed_findings` with the rule that did it as its `suppressed_reason`, still exported to SARIF marked suppressed; deleting it would mean anyone with commit access could turn a Red release Green by adding two lines and the audit trail would agree with them. It also cannot talk the gate *below* the reviewer's own verdict, because `reconcile_gate` is a floor and never a ceiling. **(2) A rule that matched nothing is reported** (`result["suppression"]["unused"]`), because the way this feature fails is a typo: the developer believes they suppressed something, the build still fails, and the tool takes the blame. **(3) The glob subset is written out rather than handed to `fnmatch`**, whose `*` matches `/` — `src/*.py` would have covered `src/a/b/c.py`, so a rule meant for one directory would quietly cover a subtree. **(4) Two file lists, not one** (`suppression.from_files`): `.lmignore` has no reviewable extension so `repo_ingest.normalize_files` drops it, and it must be looked for in the **raw** list, while inline directives are scanned from the **reviewed** list. A pull request has no file list at all, so `source_bundle.attach_repo_suppression` fetches the file at the reviewed commit — one Contents API call, failing open on the 404 that is the common case — and inline directives there are read out of the finding's own evidence quote, which is looser (a quote spans lines) but is the difference between working on pull requests and not. Landmine found while building: `".lmignore".lstrip("./")` returns `"lmignore"` — `lstrip` strips a character *set*, not a prefix — so the ignore file could not be found by its own name and no rule about any dotfile could match; `suppression._norm` is the one normalizer now. `docs/suppression.md` is the user-facing reference. |
| D-336 | 2026-09-07 | **`docs/ci/` carries a GitHub Actions and a GitLab CI workflow, both quoting the run before it happens and both publishing findings even when the gate fails.** | Two shapes in them are deliberate and easy to get wrong. **The quote runs first, on its own step, with a per-pull-request ceiling** — without it a pull request touching 800 files spends whatever it spends and the number arrives on the invoice; the monthly cap on the key is still enforced server-side, this is a second, local one. **The SARIF upload happens before the job fails** (`continue-on-error` on the review step in Actions, `artifacts: when: always` in GitLab), because failing first blocks the developer *and* hides the reason, which is how a check gets switched off rather than fixed. The GitLab file sends a locally computed `base...head` diff rather than a repository URL, because this platform fetches GitHub URLs and nothing else. What the docs say it does **not** do is as important as what it does: no inline diff annotations *from the CLI*, no incremental review *from the CLI*, no caching between runs. Both of the first two arrived later on the server side and the docs were corrected rather than left to overpromise — annotations with a GitHub App (D-343) and incremental review on a webhook push (D-341), neither of which a pipeline invocation can have, because one needs an App installation and the other needs to know which commit was last reviewed. |
| D-335 | 2026-09-07 | **`scripts/lmsdlc.py` is the CLI, and `smoke_cli.py` drives it as a subprocess against a real server.** `--files` paths are sent repository-relative. | The only ways in were the browser and hand-written `curl`; the n8n templates and the VS Code `tasks.json` are examples of writing that curl, and neither gives an exit code to gate a merge on nor an output another tool can read. Three things earn it over curl: `--fail-on red` exits 1 so the review *is* the build step; `--sarif` fetches **the server's own** export rather than building a second one that would drift (the server already decides that a discarded finding is never exported, a suppressed one is exported marked, and the fingerprint travels as `partialFingerprints`); and `--quote-only` prices the run with no model call. Driven as a *subprocess* against a *real* server because the value is entirely in what only exists end to end — an exit code, a file on disk, a key that authenticates — and two client defects were found there and nowhere else: an absolute `--files` path refused server-side as `unsafe_path` with a message about binary files, and the fix for it using `os.path.relpath`, which on Windows **raises** across drives rather than returning a `..` path, so a repository on D: and a file on C: died with a ValueError about mount points instead of the sentence written for it. |
| D-334 | 2026-09-07 | **The 23 legacy agent handlers that were `async def` and never awaited are now plain `def`, and `scripts/measure_event_loop.py` is the measurement C-31 asked for.** The three 202 job routes stay `async def`. | C-31's status said the sweep "wants measuring, not a blind edit", so it was measured first: a server with a sleeping gateway stub, one review in flight, `GET /` timed alongside it. **Before: `GET /` blocked for 5.45s of a 6s review. After: 0.30s, while the review still took 6.43s.** FastAPI runs an `async def` handler on the event loop and a plain `def` on a threadpool, so removing the keyword is the whole fix (D-228 did it for the source routes). `full_review`, `review_github_pr_async` and `review_issue_triage_batch_github_async` are excluded and named in the check: they schedule with `asyncio.create_task`, which needs a running loop, and a threadpool worker has none — flipping one would break it silently. The ~7 `/upload` variants are **still blocking**: they `await file.read()` so they cannot simply be flipped, and they call the orchestrator after the await (C-83). `smoke_products.py` §19 catches the declaration drifting back, and reports all 23 against the previous commit; it cannot measure concurrency, which is why the measurement script stays in the repository rather than being deleted as scaffolding. |
| D-333 | 2026-09-07 | **The destination radios moved from the quote panel to the preview panel.** | They were asked for *before* the patch existed, which is the wrong order: the reader chose where a change would go while having seen the cost and nothing else. The quote now names which destinations are possible as prose, and the choice is made beside the diff. An unavailable destination is still listed with its reason rather than hidden — a missing option reads as a broken screen (the same rule `fixEligibility`'s disabled checkbox follows). |
| D-332 | 2026-09-07 | **`fix_apply.apply(output="preview")` generates and guards a patch without delivering it; `fix_apply.deliver()` delivers a held one and calls no model.** The patched contents are held in-process under a single-use token (`main._FIX_PENDING`) and never returned to the client. | `apply` took the destination with it, so one call generated the patch *and* opened the pull request, and the diff appeared in the result — after the branch existed. On a platform whose whole argument is that a human approves what an agent proposes, remediation was the one write with no approval step: `plan` quoted the money beforehand and nothing showed the change beforehand. Split rather than "run with `output=diff`, then run again with `output=pr`", because that pays for the same patch twice **and can produce a different one the second time** — so the diff somebody approved would not be the diff that shipped. Contents held server-side because a client able to post file contents to the delivery route could commit anything it liked to the customer's repository under a fix's name; the token is all that crosses. `PREVIEW` is deliberately outside `OUTPUTS` so nothing enumerating deliveries has to special-case it. Delivery is `agent_guard.preflight`-checked rather than `guarded_call`-wrapped: it writes to a real repository so D-307 still applies, but there is nothing to meter and a zero-cost ledger row per delivery would only make the usage export harder to read. A refused delivery puts the patch back, so choosing again is free. |
| D-331 | 2026-09-07 | **The Findings screen gains free text, agent, repository, control-prefix and date filters, and `_finding_filter_sql` is shared by `list_findings` and `count_findings`.** | The toolbar was two `<select>`s over a table that grows without bound, and severity offered three options — so "the SQL injections in the payments service", "everything the vulnerability assessor raised this month" and "everything under SEC-004" were all unaskable, which is how a queue screen stops being opened. The filter fragment is built once because the counts are rendered *beside* the list: two copies is two chances for the header to describe a different set from the body, which is worse than either being wrong alone. `count_findings` calls it with `min_severity=0` deliberately, preserving the existing rule that the severity breakdown describes the whole status-filtered set rather than the slice on screen. `q` uses a leading-wildcard `LIKE`, which cannot use an index — accepted rather than adding an FTS5 table to maintain for a screen opened a few times a day. The agent list is filled from `GET /api/v1/products` rather than hardcoded, so it cannot become a third place agent names live. |
| D-330 | 2026-09-07 | **`renderUnifiedDiff` replaces the escaped `<pre>`, numbering lines from the `@@` hunk headers rather than by counting.** | The only diff in the application sat on the screen that asks you to read a patch before it reaches your repository, rendered as undifferentiated monospace — which makes that instruction decorative, because an uncoloured diff of any size is one nobody reads. Numbers come from the hunk headers because the old-file and new-file counters advance at different rates, which is exactly the arithmetic a reader cannot do in their head; an added line therefore shows no old-file number rather than an invented one. Deliberately unified rather than side-by-side: it is what `git` shows, what the patch actually is, and it fits beside a 220px sidebar without a scroller of its own. `color-mix` has an `@supports` floor, so an older engine keeps the row background and loses nothing — the `+`/`-` prefix is still in the text. |
| D-329 | 2026-09-07 | **A finding's `file:line` links into the existing file viewer at that line, gated on `_openFileAgent`/`_openFileRunId` rather than on the fix entitlement.** | The viewer already had a line gutter and a chip distinguishing the bytes reviewed from a refetch at the same commit, and nothing anywhere linked into it: reading a finding in context meant matching the path against the inventory table by eye. Published by the screen that painted the result, the same shape as `_fixCapable`, because `renderFinding` is called by fourteen renderers and cannot know which one it is inside. **Not** gated on the Auto-Fix entitlement — opening a file the review already read is a read of the customer's own source, not a use of a product — so a plan without Auto-Fix still sees its findings in context. |
| D-328 | 2026-09-07 | **Screens and stored runs are addressable (`#/code`, `#/code/run/<id>`, `#/findings?q=…`), through one `showPage` both the click handler and the hash listener call. The kick-off is the last statement in `app.js`.** | The application had no URL state at all: no way to send a colleague a finding, no bookmark, an inert Back button, and a reload that always landed on the Dashboard however deep you were — for a tool meant to be used daily by a team, "here, look at this" was impossible. A hash rather than the History API because the frontend is static files with no server-side rewrite, so a real path would 404 on reload. One `showPage` so a click and a pasted link cannot diverge, which is the bug that appears the first time a screen gains a loader in one path only. **Last statement in the file, not in the `// ── Init ──` block at ~line 6,400**: `_applyHash` reads `AGENT_SCREENS` and `_openRunId`, both `const` and both declared ~3,000 lines below, so calling it from there worked for `#/dashboard` and threw a temporal-dead-zone `ReferenceError` for every agent address. Routing is checked in `ui_examine.py` rather than `smoke_render.js`, because the question is whether `data-page`, `#page-<name>` and the router agree and a stub whose `querySelector` returns null cannot tell. |
| D-327 | 2026-09-07 | **`finding_pipeline.resolved_since` credits a finding as fixed only in a file the run actually read**, and reports `unverifiable_count` for the rest. | `apply_baseline` labelled what is *present*; nothing labelled what is *gone*, so a fix and a still-open finding were the same absence — no burn-down, no fix rate, no time-to-remediate, and no way to say "this change closed four and introduced one". The file restriction is the whole design: without it a review of one file reports every finding in every file it did not open as fixed, which is a false all-clear that looks like progress. `files_read` comes from the coverage record, so a file in a *failed* chunk is correctly excluded; a single pasted artifact has no paths and credits nothing, saying so as `scope_limited` rather than guessing. A finding with no `file_path` is never credited — it is about the design, not a line. `mark_findings_resolved` writes `status='resolved'`, deliberately **not** in `WAIVED_STATUSES`, so a finding that returns is reported as a regression rather than silently muted; and it updates only `status='open'` rows, so a deliberate waiver is never converted into "fixed". |
| D-326 | 2026-09-07 | **`reviews` gains `repo_host`/`repo_owner`/`repo_name`/`branch`/`commit_sha`/`pr_number`, denormalized rather than joined from `review_sources`, and existing rows are backfilled from it.** | `reviews` could say what a run found and what it cost and never *where*, so every question about a place was unanswerable by query — is this repository improving, what has it cost us, which is worst. Denormalized because `review_sources` rows exist only for the two products that keep a re-readable source, while `github_repo_url` is a declared source for eight: putting it on the row every product writes means a repository view covers whatever reviews a repo later, with no second migration. Backfilled because a repository view whose history starts today cannot show a trend, and the trend is the point. `Bundle.source_ref()` normalizes the three GitHub shapes (a repo's `ref`, a PR's `head_branch`, a compare's `head`) in one place, and `context.bind_source` carries it to `save_review` by the same route as `project_id` and D-323's actor. NULL for a paste or an upload, so the repository index never holds a row for one. `database.list_repositories` is the query it exists for. |
| D-325 | 2026-09-07 | **`fix_apply.apply` resolves `owner`/`repo`/`gh` before the `base_ref` block that uses them**, and `smoke_review_sources.py` §31 now checks that ordering structurally with an AST walk. | The default-branch fallback called `gh.get_default_branch(owner, repo)` *above* the three lines assigning those names. All three are assigned later in the same function, so Python treated them as locals throughout it and the call raised `UnboundLocalError` — the sentence written to replace an opaque 422 (D-315) was itself a traceback. Third defect in the same twenty lines, and unreachable by every existing test because they all stop at `plan`, which writes nothing (D-317). Checked by walking the parsed function and requiring each name's first read to follow its first binding, which catches the class rather than the instance — and over `ast.unparse` output, not raw source, because the first version of that check compared string offsets and was fooled by a comment mentioning the function it was looking for. |
| D-324 | 2026-09-07 | **`CORS_ALLOW_ORIGINS` replaces `allow_origins=["*"]`, and `config.assert_production_secrets()` refuses to boot on the shipped `SECRET_KEY` or `DEFAULT_ADMIN_PASSWORD` when `APP_ENV=production`.** | Starlette echoes the requesting origin back when `allow_origins=["*"]` and `allow_credentials=True` are both set, so any web page could make credentialed calls here — which mattered most for the routes in D-318 that needed no credential at all: it turned a network-local problem into one a malicious page could trigger from a colleague's browser. Default empty because the bundled SPA is same-origin and never needed it; `X-API-Key` callers are unaffected, CORS being a browser mechanism. The secret check is fatal rather than a warning because `SECRET_KEY` both signs every JWT *and* derives the single Fernet key for every stored credential (`crypto_service.py:15`), so on the default value a copy of this repository decrypts every GitHub token in the database — and a startup warning is a warning nobody reads. Gated on `APP_ENV=production` so the smoke suites and `ui_examine.py`, which rely on both defaults deliberately, are untouched. |
| D-323 | 2026-09-07 | **The request's actor is published on `backend/context.py` (`bind_project(..., actor=)`, `current_actor()`), and `review_created` / `approval_created` name it instead of defaulting to `'system'`.** | The audit trail's whole purpose is "who did what", and it could not answer it for the one event type it always wrote: `save_review` had no actor to pass, so every review in the trail since the first schema was attributed to `system`, and the real answer was only reachable by joining `usage_events.caller_ref`, which no endpoint does. Same route and same reasoning as `project_id` and `product_id` already travelling this way — the boundary knows the caller, and threading a parameter through 19 `save_review` call sites is exactly how attribution got lost the first time. `agent_guard` binds `caller_ref` (already computed by `_caller_identity`); `legacy_guard` gets `_actor_for(subject)`, written locally rather than imported because that middleware only ever holds a resolved `Subject`, never a caller dict. Still falls back to `'system'` outside any request, which is honest — nobody ran those. |
| D-322 | 2026-09-07 | **`audit_trail` and `ai_systems` gain `project_id`; `_review_scope_sql` is renamed `_project_scope_sql`; `audit_trail` gains its first indexes.** | These were the two governance surfaces `scope.py` had never been applied to (D-42), and the reason was not oversight in the router — there was no column to filter on, so adding the dependency alone could not have scoped them. NULL on every pre-existing row, and `project_id IN (...)` never matches NULL, so historical rows and genuinely deployment-wide events (`user_login`, `plan_created`) stay super-admin-only, exactly as `reviews`/`reports`/`approvals` already handle theirs. Renamed because the helper does nothing review-specific and is now used by six tables; a `_review_`-prefixed helper on `audit_trail` reads as though the other tables need their own. Indexed because `list_audit_events` did an unindexed `actor LIKE '%…%'` plus an `ORDER BY` over a table that only ever grows. |
| D-321 | 2026-09-07 | **`routes/modernization.answer_checkpoint` authorizes before it writes, via the new `store.get_checkpoint`.** | It called `store.answer_checkpoint` first — which commits — and `_require_run` second, so any caller holding any valid credential could answer a checkpoint in **any** project's run given only its id: they received a 404 and the row was already changed with their name in `answered_by`. Answering `build_required` or `parity_divergence` is what lets a run finish without ever being compiled or replayed (`runner.py:3664`), so this was a cross-tenant write against a governance gate. Every other route in that module already authorized first (`:858`, `:864`, `:873`). **The test that catches it asserts the row's state, not the status code** — 404 was returned before the fix and after it (`smoke_modernization.py`, beside "answering a checkpoint over HTTP works"). |
| D-320 | 2026-09-07 | **A Knowledge Base write is gated by *who owns the prompt it changes*: a shared document needs a super admin, a project's document needs an admin of that project, and an API key is refused outright.** | Not the usual "authenticated is enough" bar, because a KB document is not data — `orchestrator._kb()` folds it into the prompt of every later review in its category, so it sets the standing instructions an agent is judged by. A shared document (`project_name` empty) reaches every project in the deployment, which is why that is the one bar that could not be relaxed without recreating D-318. Project admin rather than membership for the rest: this is configuration, closer to setting a budget than to running a review. An API key is refused because it has no notion of administering a project, and letting a CI credential rewrite the rules its own reviews are graded against is the same hole wearing a credential. The write is now audited (`kb_document_uploaded`/`_updated`/`_deleted`), which nothing recorded before — a review that changed behaviour because somebody edited its knowledge base looked identical to one that changed because the code did. |
| D-319 | 2026-09-07 | **`_kb_require_writable` maps the caller's project *ids* to slugs rather than trusting the `project_name` it was handed.** | KB paths are keyed by `slugify_project(project_name)` and `project_name` is free text on the request, so checking the string against itself would authorize nothing. The check resolves `database.list_admin_project_ids` to project rows to slugs and compares; reads use `scope.visible_project_ids` through the same mapping. This is also why the KB could not simply reuse `scope.visible_from_caller` the way `findings.py` does — every other governance surface is keyed by `project_id` and this one is keyed by a slug of the name. |
| D-318 | 2026-09-07 | **Five endpoints had no authorization and now do: all five `/api/knowledge-base` routes, all six `/api/inventory` routes, `GET /api/audit`, `POST /api/v1/modernization/checkpoints/{id}/answer` (ordering, D-321), and `GET /api/v1/issue-triage/dashboard` (authenticated but unscoped).** | Found while reviewing enterprise readiness, not by a test — nothing in the suite asserted that an unauthenticated call is refused, so `smoke_products.py` §18 now does, for each of them, and every check in it fails on the previous commit (verified: `git show HEAD:` on all three routers returns **zero** `Depends(`). The Knowledge Base was much the worst: `POST` was an anonymous write into `docs/<category>/` whose content reaches every subsequent review's prompt, so a caller could leave standing instructions to ignore a vulnerability class or report Green and the run would look entirely normal — the exact attack the Prompt & Agent Safety product sells detection for. `/api/inventory` exposed `owner`/`team`/`data_classification`/`compliance_frameworks` deployment-wide, `DELETE` included (a soft delete to `Decommissioned`, not a row removal). The triage dashboard filtered on free-text `project_name` only, so omitting it aggregated every customer's triage reviews for any signed-in user. |
| D-317 | 2026-09-04 | ~~Landmine: no automated test reaches Apply Fixes' write path, and two bugs lived there.~~ **Closed 2026-09-08.** Six in the end (D-314, D-315, D-325, and the three in D-364), and the last three were found by `verify_fix_apply.py`'s first live run — one of which had left the whole feature non-functional for eight commits. Still true of the *offline* net, which is why D-363 now guards the cheapest class of it. `smoke_review_sources.py` section 31 now guards the arguments each one got wrong. | The section can only check the *arguments*, not the outcome — proving a branch and a pull request appear needs a real repository, which spends money and writes to GitHub. So the offline net is as strong as it can be here and no stronger, and the residual risk is stated rather than papered over: `scripts/verify_pr_review.py` covers PR *review* writes and has no equivalent for fixes. | D-310 | 2026-09-04 | **A stored run's cost is READ from `usage_events`, never copied onto the `reviews` row.** `database.get_review_spend(ids)` sums the ledger; `GET /api/reviews/{id}` and `GET /api/reviews` both serve it as `spend`. | The cost could never have travelled inside `result_json`: it is not known until the model calls finish, which is after `save_review` has written and serialized the row — which is why reopening a run showed findings with no cost. Storing a copy on the review would put a second billing figure in a second table, free to disagree with the invoice; the ledger row is the one CLAUDE.md already calls authoritative. Two consequences worth knowing: the async and `/api/v1` paths gain this **retroactively**, because `agent_guard._record_usage_event` has always passed `result["review_id"]`; and a run with no ledger row is **absent from the result, not present with zeroes** — it cost an unknown amount, and a confident $0.00 beside it is a false statement about money. Summed rather than read from one row because a failed-then-retried run leaves a non-billable row beside the billable one, so `billable_cost_usd` is reported apart from `cost_usd`. |
| D-311 | 2026-09-04 | **`context.collect_saved_reviews()` reports review ids back *up* to the metering middleware, using a mutable list rather than a value** (`backend/context.py`, read in `legacy_guard`). | Everything else in `context.py` travels downward — the boundary publishes the project and `save_review` reads it. This is the reverse, and it needs the same trick `llm_gateway.track_usage()` uses: a synchronous FastAPI endpoint runs in a worker thread with a **copy** of the caller's context, so `ContextVar.set()` inside it is invisible to the middleware outside. Appending to a list the parent still references is visible because it is the same object. A plain `set()` would have failed silently on exactly the routes this exists for — every agent screen but two — so the check for it drives a real `copy_context().run(...)`. Where a request saves several reviews the **last** is the one named: a composite writes each stage and then its own on top, so the last is the one the caller was handed and the one a reader reopens. The stages carry no cost of their own, which is honest — they were parts of one metered call, not separately metered. |
| D-312 | 2026-09-04 | **Landmine: `scripts/ui_examine.py`'s gateway stub now calls `llm_gateway._record_usage`.** | It did not, and could not have: `_record_usage` lives *inside* the real `call_tool`, so replacing that function replaced the metering with it. Every review in every browser examination therefore cost zero calls, and **nothing about money reaching a screen was checkable at all** — not the spend strip, not the cost on a history card, not the figure on a reopened run. The same blind spot existed in the offline suites until `smoke_review_sources.py` section 27 wrapped the stub the same way. If a future stub replaces `call_tool` again, it has to keep this line or that coverage silently returns to zero. |
| D-313 | 2026-09-04 | **Landmine: `scripts/smoke_render.js`'s paging section replaced `sandbox.fetch` and never restored it.** Now saved and restored in a `finally`, with a check asserting the shared stub is still installed. | Two other places in that file swap `sandbox.fetch` temporarily and restore it; this one did not, so **every check after it read one specific stale fixture** — `fakePage(0, 4, 4)` — no matter what it had just assigned to `nextFetchBody`. Silent, because a page of reviews from an unrelated test is still a plausible-looking response: the first tests to depend on it failed with the app looking wrong and the harness being wrong, which cost real time to tell apart. The guard check exists so the next such omission names itself. |
| D-297 | 2026-09-04 | **Apply Fixes works from a VAPT assessment.** No new endpoint, no new product: `renderVAPTResult` now calls the same `armResultActions` the code path does (`app.js`), and `renderVAPTFinding` renders the fix checkbox. | The backend was already review-type agnostic — `/api/reviews/{id}/fix` takes any review in scope and never looked at `review_type`, and a VAPT assessment runs through the same `_run_bundle_review` → `_record_sources` path a code review does, so its source was recorded and re-readable all along. The only thing missing was a renderer that armed it. Delivery therefore falls out of `_available_outputs` unchanged: commit into the pull request under review, a new pull request, a branch, a zip, or the diff. |
| D-298 | 2026-09-04 | **`armResultActions` is the one place that decides whether a result can be fixed** (`app.js`, called by `renderResult` and `renderVAPTResult`). | The last three defects on this path were all of the form "the other renderer does it differently" — the inventory painted into the wrong screen, a reopened assessment rendered through the plain renderer, the fix bar armed where nothing could be patched.
