| D-299 | 2026-09-04 | **`#vapt-sources` was a container nothing ever filled.** Fixed by the same call. | `loadReviewSources` had exactly two call sites and `renderVAPTResult` was neither, so which files an assessment read, which it skipped and why, and the retention control were invisible on the screen that produced them — for as long as the panel has existed. Nothing offline could see it: the DOM-stubbed renderer test asserts on the HTML a renderer returns, and this panel is filled by a fetch that happens after it returns. `ui_examine.py` section 13c is the check that can. |
| D-300 | 2026-09-04 | **The fix checkbox goes in the VAPT card's header, via `renderFixCheckbox(f, 'compact')`; the *reason* a vulnerability cannot be fixed stays in the body, via `'reasons'`.** | VAPT cards are collapsed by default (`toggleVAPT`), so a box in the body needs twelve cards expanded to tick three. The two variants exist so one finding can never end up with two checkboxes carrying the same id — which is what happened first, because the default variant renders a tickable box too. The header label calls `event.stopPropagation()` or ticking also collapses the card being read. Default behaviour is untouched, so the seven screens already calling it do not change. |
| D-301 | 2026-09-04 | **A patch is re-reviewed by the agent that found the problem** — `_verify_patch(..., review_agent=)` routes to `orch.vapt_agent.run_vapt` for a VAPT fix, `orch.code_agent.review_code` otherwise (`autofix_pr.py`). `fix_apply._review_agent` is the single mapping. | `fix_apply`'s own `verification_note` promises the patch was "re-reviewed by the same reviewer that found the original problem", which was untrue for a VAPT fix. Beyond honesty: a code reviewer asked whether an attack path is closed is being asked a question it was not built for. One mapping rather than two so the quote and the guardrail cannot disagree about which agent a review belongs to — if they did, a run would be checked by one agent and billed against another. The default keeps Auto-Fix on a pull request byte-identical, and a check asserts that specifically. |
| D-302 | 2026-09-04 | **Only a Critical the original review did *not* report blocks a patch.** `_verify_patch` takes `known_fingerprints`; `_guard_patches` groups them **per file**. Omit them and every Critical blocks, as before. | Not a loosening — it is what the check's own docstring always claimed to do ("a *new* Critical in code the tool just wrote"). Any-Critical-blocks was survivable on code review and would have rejected almost every VAPT patch: fix one of three vulnerabilities in a file and the re-review truthfully reports the other two, both Critical, and the good patch is thrown away. Grouped per file because an old vulnerability in `a.py` says nothing about a new one in `b.py`. **The weakness is the fingerprint**: `finding_pipeline.fingerprint` includes the model-written title, so a re-worded description of the same vulnerability will not match and the patch is rejected — the safe direction, and why a rejection says "the original review did not report" rather than asserting the fixer introduced it. Criticals that were carried over are reported in `carried_over` / `carried_over_criticals`, because passing the guard is not the same as the file being clean. |
| D-303 | 2026-09-04 | **A fix is quoted as two halves, priced against two agents** — `review_estimate.combine(for_chunks(agent='auto-fix'), for_chunks(agent=<reviewer>))` in `fix_apply.plan`. `_frame_tokens` gained an `auto-fix` branch and `MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS['auto-fix']` went 16000 → 32000. | The single `for_chunks(agent="code", extra_calls=n)` was wrong twice, both times under-quoting: it priced the *fixer's* call against the code reviewer's prompt frame and 16k output ceiling (a fix returns a whole rewritten file and asks for 32k, per `AutoFixAgent.MAX_OUTPUT_TOKENS`), and it counted each file's content once for two calls that each carry it in full. Measured effect on a two-file code fix: expected $0.2406 → $0.2369 (the fixer's frame is *smaller* than the reviewer's, 1221 vs 3975 tokens), ceiling $1.02 → $1.50. **Correcting an earlier guess: the VAPT frame is smaller than the code reviewer's (3416 vs 3975), not larger** — so a VAPT fix quotes marginally *below* a code fix on the same file, and the test asserts the frame arithmetic rather than a direction. |
| D-304 | 2026-09-04 | **The fix bar is removed while its own quote is on screen** (`refreshFixBar`, `planFix`, `closeFixPanel` in `app.js`). | The bar is `position: fixed` at the bottom of the viewport and the quote panel is tall, so the panel's "Fix them" and "Cancel" buttons came to rest underneath it and a click on either landed on the bar — on the Code Review path as much as VAPT. Padding below the actions only moved where it happened; two competing action rows was the real problem. Cancel restores the bar and **keeps the ticks**: cancelling a quote means "not at this price", not "forget which vulnerabilities I picked". Found by driving a real browser — both elements are correct in isolation and only their stacking is wrong, so nothing that reads back rendered HTML could see it. |
| D-305 | 2026-09-04 | **The fixer is told the CWE, the OWASP category, the attack scenario and the proof of concept** (`autofix_agent._describe_finding`), all conditional on the key being present. | Only the vulnerability assessor sets them, so a code-review finding's prompt is byte-identical — asserted against an exact string, because "unchanged" is the claim most easily broken by accident. Worth the tokens: a patch that sanitises the wrong end of the path closes nothing, and the attack scenario is the only field that says which end is which. The proof of concept is capped at six lines and 600 characters for the same reason `evidence` is capped — it can be a multi-step script and the call already carries a whole file. |
| D-306 | 2026-09-04 | **The proof of concept and the CVSS vector were each rendered twice in every VAPT card** — the detail grid plus an older inline-styled copy below it. The second copy is gone. | A duplicate reads as a long card rather than as a bug, and the two copies were far enough apart in the source to look like different fields. Found by deleting the grid copy during a breakage pass and watching a check that should have failed pass instead: the second copy was silently keeping it green. `smoke_render.js` now asserts each appears exactly once. |
| D-307 | 2026-09-04 | **Applying fixes stays part of the `auto-fix` product, whichever review the findings came from.** Decided 2026-09-04. A project without it sees the scan, the findings and the waivers, and one line saying fixing is not in its plan (`armResultActions` → `renderFixUnavailable`, `app.js`). | The alternative — letting a VAPT entitlement cover its own fixes — was considered and rejected: fixing writes to real repositories and spends real money, so it is gated behind its own purchase, and `usage_events` keeps attributing every repository write to `auto-fix` rather than splitting the same action across two product names depending on which screen started it. The consequence to accept is that a customer who bought a security scan cannot act on it without a second purchase; the mitigation is that they are told before they select anything, not after the quote. Selling fix-for-security separately was the third option and was rejected as new product surface (a registry entry, its own quota, and every products listing) for a question that does not need it yet. |
| D-308 | 2026-09-04 | **A synchronous review now refreshes its own screen's run history** (`runReviewFromQuote` calls `loadAgentRuns`). | The async path did this from `pollReviewJob`; the small-run path never did, so a review under the async threshold left "Previous runs" a run behind until you navigated away and came back. Trivial, but it made the list look broken on exactly the runs that finish fast enough for someone to look at it immediately. |
| D-309 | 2026-09-04 | **Landmine: a breakage harness must snapshot bytes, not `git checkout --`.** | The first version of this session's harness restored each mutated file with `git checkout -- <path>`, which restores from HEAD and therefore **deleted the uncommitted work being tested** — all of `fix_apply.py`'s changes, silently, on the first case. It was recoverable only because the harness crashed on its own reporting bug before reaching case two. Snapshot the exact bytes and write them back in a `finally`; git is not the source of truth for a dirty tree. |
| D-296 | 2026-09-03 | **The governance report exports as Markdown** — `governanceReportMarkdown(r)`, a pure function, with `downloadGovernanceReport()` wrapping it. | The screen calls itself the audit-ready report and offered no way to hand it to an auditor: the only affordance was a report id, which is a UUID nobody can act on outside this application. Markdown rather than PDF because it is what the rest of this codebase already exports (`MODERNIZATION_REPORT.md`, the GxP documents) and because a diffable artifact is worth more in a governance trail than a rendered one. **The export states a finding's waiver status, who set it and when it expires** — without that, a risk somebody deliberately accepted exports as an open problem, which is the opposite of what the waiver recorded. Split into a pure function so what it writes is asserted without a browser or a file; the checks include that the export's order matches the screen's, so the two cannot disagree about what matters most. |
| D-293 | 2026-09-03 | **The Core SDLC Review's stage label goes into the result panel, not a blocking modal.** `renderReviewProgress` gained an optional `title`. | It is the highest-call-count workflow here — up to seven sequential model calls, routinely over five minutes — and it had **already** reported a real stage label per step since it shipped. That label went into `showLoading`, a full-screen overlay: one line of text on top of the page, with the form, the run history and the previous result unreachable behind it, on the run most likely to make someone want to look at something else. Nothing new had to be computed; the label just needed somewhere honest to go. Using the same panel as the review screens means one set of wording and one place to fix it. |
| D-294 | 2026-09-03 | **`POST /api/v1/swarm/async` and `POST /api/review/issue-triage/batch-github/async`** — 202 plus a job id, polled at `/api/review/jobs/{id}`. The swarm runs three to six agents in parallel; progress is reported **as agents finish, naming the one that just landed**, because they run concurrently so a start order says nothing about how much is left, and a bare "4 of 6" cannot tell a swarm that is progressing from one where the slow agent is still the slow agent. The repository triage is **serial by design** — each issue re-reads the project's triage history so later issues can be matched as duplicates of earlier ones — so forty issues is forty sequential calls, and the label names the issue being read. Both are deliberately absent from `legacy_guard.LEGACY_PRODUCT_ROUTES` for the reason the other 202 routes are: the work happens after the response is sent, so `guarded_call` inside the job is what meters it, and `preflight` runs before the 202 so an over-budget caller gets 402/403/429 rather than an accepted job that fails a minute later. The sync twins stay because they are the documented API surface (D-269's reasoning, applied twice more). Swarm's validation and its `llm_used` summarisation were extracted so the twin cannot drift from it on what produced the findings, and its initial `total_steps` is the policy's agent count — an upper bound knowable without a GitHub round trip — which the first progress report revises downward before the bar has moved. |
| D-295 | 2026-09-03 | **`codegen-scaffold` and `migration-execute` are deliberately given no progress bar.** A check asserts they take no `on_progress`. | Both were on the list of slow screens to fix, and both turned out to make **one** agent call (`codegen_scaffold.py:29`, `migration_execute.py:26`). A bar over a single call goes from 0 to 100 with an invented label in between; a spinner at least does not claim to know anything. The check is written as an assertion rather than a comment so that if either ever fans out, the test says so instead of the omission quietly becoming wrong. |
| D-292 | 2026-09-03 | **A finding can be waived from the screen that reported it on every screen that reports one** — `renderWaiveControl` is now called from the four bespoke finding renderers as well as from `renderFinding`. | It was called from exactly one place, inside the shared `renderFinding`, so a screen with its own per-finding markup showed findings nobody could waive, resolve, accept the risk on or report as a false positive without going to the separate Findings screen and locating them there by hand. **VAPT was the worst case because `index.html:1433` says "Findings can be waived"** — the screen made a promise its renderer did not keep. Four were affected: VAPT, Sensitive Data, 21 CFR Part 11, GxP Document Check. Everything else already routed through `renderFinding`, Security and Architecture included — `renderFindingCard` is a one-line delegation to it, which is why they were fine and looked like they might not be. **Fixed by calling the control from each renderer rather than routing them through the shared one:** VAPT's own renderer is the only thing that shows the CVSS vector, the attack scenario and the proof of concept, and trading those for a button would be a bad deal. The plan for this work assumed `renderFinding` would need an extension point and called it the riskiest edit; it did not, because the control was already a standalone function. |
| D-290 | 2026-09-03 | **Each agent screen shows the frameworks its findings map to and where the month's allowance stands**, from the catalogue `refreshEntitlements` already fetches (`renderAgentAbout`, inserted after each `.page-header`). | `GET /api/v1/products` already returns, per product, the frameworks, whether this project's plan allows it, and the calls and dollars used this period (`routes/products.py:35-66`) — and `refreshEntitlements` threw all of it away except the entitled/not-entitled bit, so it was rendered only on the admin-only Plans screen. A user out of quota, or on a plan that never included this agent, found out by filling in the form, pressing the button and being refused. Costs no extra request and refreshes on the same events. Inserted into the DOM rather than given nineteen markup slots, because one id per screen is one more thing to keep in step with `AGENT_SCREENS`. **A null usage is not a zero:** `used_*` come back null with no project or no entitlement, and "0 of 100 used" for a lookup that was never made is a number we invented. |
| D-291 | 2026-09-03 | **Landmine: `Product.sources` documents the `/api/v1` endpoint's parameter names, not what a screen accepts.** Deliberately not rendered on the agent screens. | It reads exactly like "what this agent accepts" and is not. It was **already entirely screen-agnostic** and was called from two renderers out of sixteen, so seventeen screens computed all of it and rendered none of it. The insertion point was found by counting div depth from each header rather than by fourteen hand-written anchors, which is fourteen chances to put it somewhere wrong. The check is a loop over the live `AGENT_SCREENS` table, so a nineteenth screen is covered without editing the test; proven by removing the block from one renderer and watching three checks fail, naming that screen. |
| D-285 | 2026-09-03 | **The SARIF export is offered only where the result has findings.** | `sarif.from_review` exports any review (`findings.py:213`), and the transparency block is now on the generative screens too — so *Download as SARIF* appeared under a set of generated test cases and a stack recommendation, where it downloads an empty file. Suppressed findings count toward the gate on this: a review whose only findings are in test paths still has a SARIF file worth exporting, and they are included, marked suppressed. | D-286 | 2026-09-03 | **What a synchronous run cost comes back in `X-Spend-*` response headers** (`legacy_guard.py`), folded into `_budget_stats` by `apiFetch._foldSpendHeaders` so the existing spend strip renders unchanged. | `agent_guard.guarded_call` attaches cost, calls, tokens, model and provider to the response *body*. This middleware cannot: it runs **around** the handler, so by the time the numbers exist `call_next` has already serialized the body and there is no key left to add. Every agent screen except Code Review and VAPT therefore showed findings with no indication of what producing them cost or which model produced them — all of it computed, written to `usage_events`, and thrown away. Headers are the one channel still open after `call_next`, which the rate-limit block four lines above already relies on. **The figures come from `agent_guard._price_usage`, the same arithmetic that writes the ledger** — a display cost derived any other way drifts from the invoice, which is worse than showing nothing, and there is a check comparing the two. A body that already has `_budget_stats` wins: that path carries the remaining monthly budget and the per-project figures, and overwriting it with the header subset would be a downgrade dressed as a fix. A zero-call response is dropped rather than rendered — "cost $0.0000" on a free preflight is noise pretending to be transparency. |
| D-287 | 2026-09-03 | **`CORSMiddleware` now lists `expose_headers`** (`main.py`) — the spend headers and the four rate-limit ones. | Only six response headers are readable by JavaScript on a cross-origin request unless they are named here. The bundled SPA is same-origin so it never needed the permission, which is why the list did not exist — but that also meant **`X-RateLimit-Remaining-*` has always been invisible to any client hosted elsewhere**, so a caller could not see how much budget it had left in order to back off. Both sets of headers exist to be read. This grants no access to anything the response body did not already contain. |
| D-288 | 2026-09-03 | **Landmine: the offline suites had never exercised metering at all.** Section 27 of `smoke_review_sources.py` wraps the gateway stub so it calls `llm_gateway._record_usage`, then restores it. | The suites stub `call_tool`, and `_record_usage` is called *inside* the real one — so `get_tracked_usage()` returns zero calls for every run in every test, the middleware returns early at `if not usage["calls"]`, and none of the pricing, ledger or header code below it had ever run under test. Worth knowing before trusting a green suite about anything cost-related. The wrap is deliberately scoped to one section and restored in a `finally`, because a stub that records usage everywhere would change what the budget and quota assertions elsewhere are measuring. |
| D-289 | 2026-09-03 | **Every product must report its spend by one of three mechanisms, and a check names any that does not** (`smoke_review_sources.py` section 27). | The three are: in `LEGACY_PRODUCT_ROUTES` (or reachable through the universal runner regex beside it) → the headers; a 202 job or an `/api/v1` route → `_budget_stats` on the body, since those do their model work after the response is sent and leave nothing request-scoped to meter; or its own accounting, which is modernization. Two corrections the check itself forced: **`compliance` is metered by the `/api/agents/{id}/run` regex, not the route dict** — the first version of the audit knew only about the dict and reported the one product with no screen as unmetered when it is not; and **`full` is metered twice**, by the body at `POST /api/review/full` and by the headers at `POST /api/agents/full/run`, so the exemption list is compared against the dict alone rather than the union. |
| D-274 | 2026-09-03 | **Every agent screen declares itself in one table, `AGENT_SCREENS` (`app.js`), and an offline check fails if it drifts** — `smoke_review_sources.py` section 26 compares every row against `GET /api/v1/products` and against the nav's `data-product`, and fails on an agent screen with no row. | Nineteen of twenty-one agent screens had no run history, no cost, no model and no coverage — not because the data was missing but because `loadAgentRuns` was gated by a two-key literal `{code, vapt}` and one `agent === 'vapt'` ternary. Three facts a generic helper needs cannot be derived: the result container ids follow no convention (`req-result`, `sd-result`, `gxg-plan-result`), a renderer is a function so markup cannot hold one, and the history has to work when the nav item was not what opened the screen. The table duplicates product ids and review types that already live in `products/registry.py`, which is a real cost — **the check is what makes it defensible, and it is worth more than the table is.** `registry.to_public_dict` now serves `review_type` so the check compares against the served contract instead of re-parsing Python, which is how a check starts agreeing with a bug. Proven by breaking a review type, deleting a row and renaming a container: one specific failure each. |
| D-275 | 2026-09-03 | **`legacy_guard` publishes `product_id`** (`legacy_guard.py:272`, beside the `product` it already resolved two lines earlier). | `agent_guard.guarded_call` published it (D-265) and the *synchronous* middleware did not — so every run served by `/api/review/*`, which is **every agent screen except Code Review and VAPT**, stored `product_id = NULL`. A per-agent history had only `review_type` to match on, and each row was labelled *"before agents were recorded"* on a screen where the agent was perfectly well known. `review_type` is still sent alongside, because rows written before this genuinely have no product and dropping them would make an existing project's history read as empty — which looks exactly like data loss. |
| D-276 | 2026-09-03 | **`renderResult` asks which screen owns a result (`screenForReview`) instead of guessing.** | It inferred `'code'` for anything that was not VAPT, and `#code-sources` exists on every page load — so running Requirement Review, Prompt Safety or the Release Gate fetched *that* review's inventory and painted it into the **Code Review screen's** panel. Silent, because the panel being overwritten was on a screen you were not looking at. The check that catches it (`smoke_render.js`, 'One screen does not paint into another') has to await a turn of the event loop, because the fetch is a `setTimeout(..., 0)` — which is precisely why nothing saw it: the only suite that could was not waiting. |
| D-277 | 2026-09-03 | **The Apply Fixes checkbox renders only where the review has a recorded source** (`_fixCapable`, set from the owning screen's `inventory` flag). | **Policy decision, resolved 2026-09-03: hide it rather than extend source recording.** The bar armed on every agent's findings, but applying a fix needs the source read back — from the per-run store or from GitHub at the recorded sha — and only the review pipeline records one (`review_source_store.record`, called from `_record_sources`). On seventeen screens the checkbox was tickable and guaranteed to fail at `fix_apply.plan`. Same rule as D-268. **Not even the disabled "cannot be fixed" box is shown:** twenty of those explain a limitation of the screen rather than of the finding, and the waiver controls beside them are the action actually available. Making fixes genuinely work elsewhere would mean recording a source on the file-taking agents and a new `auto-fix` billable path on screens that never had one — deliberately not done. |
| D-278 | 2026-09-03 | **A stored run reopens through the screen's own renderer** (`AGENT_SCREENS[agent].render` in `openAgentRun`), and `openAgentRun` reads `spec.result` rather than `${agent}-result`. | D-261 claimed a reopened run renders through the same function as a fresh one so a reader is never comparing two differently-shaped accounts of the same thing. **For VAPT that was false:** a fresh assessment went through `renderVAPTResult` with its CVSS, CWE, attack scenario and proof of concept; reopening the same run called `renderResult`, which renders none of them. The `${agent}-result` derivation was the other half of the same assumption — it only ever resolved on the two screens whose ids happened to match the pattern. |
| D-279 | 2026-09-03 | **`_runsOffset`, `_openRunId` and the review poll timer are per-screen, not one global each.** | `_runsOffset` was a two-key literal, so any other screen computed `undefined + 10` = `NaN`, sent `offset=NaN`, and got a 422 — a history that is *broken* rather than empty, which reads the same to a user. `_openRunId` was one global two screens shared, so whichever painted last stole the other's highlight. `_reviewPollTimer` was one interval for the whole application: a run started on a second screen cleared the first screen's poller, leaving its panel frozen on the last progress it painted — a review that looks hung while it is in fact finishing normally. |
| D-280 | 2026-09-03 | **The Agent Hub's per-card Project Name box became a hidden input and a caption** (`app.js`, `loadAgentHub`). | D-250 converted the typed box on all twenty-one *static* screens. This one is built in JavaScript at runtime and was missed — for as long as no test ever opened that screen. Its own `title` attribute read *"Set by the project selector in the sidebar"*, which is an admission that a typed box was the wrong control. Found by extending `ui_examine` section 11 from *"four screens load"* to a walk over every screen in `AGENT_SCREENS`; the pre-existing check that no typed box survives had been passing because nothing had ever navigated there. **Its failure detail was also wrong** — it built the selector with literal quote characters, so it always reported "0 left". |
| D-281 | 2026-09-03 | **The project caption promises a working cost estimate only on screens that have one.** | The caption is shared by every screen and said *"Estimating a cost works without one; running a review does not"* — added by D-250 for a real user who got a successful estimate and would have hit a refusal on the run. On the seventeen screens with no quote step it describes a button that is not there. Asked of the DOM (`[id$="-cost-preview"]` within the enclosing `.page`) rather than declared, so it becomes true on its own if a screen gains a quote. |
| D-282 | 2026-09-03 | **`reviewGithubPR` and `autoFixGithubPR` deleted** (93 lines, `app.js`). | Dead since the review screens merged (D-267) — referenced from nowhere in either file, and reading `#gh-url` / `#gh-jira` / `#gh-result`, none of which exist any more. They still held live calls to `POST /api/github/pr` and `/auto-fix`, so a grep for those endpoints found frontend callers that could never run. |
| D-283 | 2026-09-03 | **Known gap: the Compliance Mapper product has no screen.** No nav item, no `#page-*` container; reachable only as an Agent Hub card (`AGENT_DEFS`). | **Policy decision, resolved 2026-09-03: leave it, record it.** It persists reviews with `review_type="compliance"` like any other agent, so a screen would get a working run history the moment it existed. Not built because adding a screen is new surface rather than visibility on existing ones. Anyone reaching for it should know it is a deliberate absence, not an oversight. |
| D-273 | 2026-09-03 | **`Bundle.files_reviewed` counts the customer's files, not our artifacts** — `files_covered`, with `artifacts` kept beside it. | The quote read **"WILL BE REVIEWED: 1 file(s)"** directly above **"12 file(s) will be read — see which"**, on the same panel. Both true about different things: a diff is one artifact to the chunker and twelve files to the person, and only the second is a statement anybody asked for. "1 file(s)" on a twelve-file pull request reads as *it will only look at one of them*, which is the exact doubt this work exists to remove. Fixed at the source rather than in the tile, because four places read it (the quote, the progress panel, the result's source line, SARIF's `filesReviewed`) and all four said 1. `artifacts` is kept because the arithmetic needs it — one artifact is one chunk is one model call, which is how a quote can honestly say "12 files, 1 model call". **The whole suite passed while this was on screen**, so the new check asserts the tile equals the length of the list beneath it, for every source shape; verified by reverting the fix and watching it fail. Also: the retention dropdown is now *disabled* for a GitHub source instead of offering a choice above a sentence saying it is not used. |
| D-267 | 2026-09-03 | **One review screen. The GitHub PR nav item is an entry point into it, not a second implementation** — post-back options appear when the source is a pull request, exactly as the token block already did. | Two screens reviewed the same artifact and the only real difference was *whether the result is written back to GitHub*, which is a checkbox. Everything else was a feature one had and the other lacked because it had only ever been built once: the token field, the cost quote, progress, run history, the source inventory, the staged fix. **Both defects a user reported this week were that divergence showing** — the PR screen had no token input, and the fix controls added to the shared finding renderer could not work there. **The products stay apart:** a run that will write goes to `/api/github/pr/async` (product `github-pr-review`), one that will not goes to `/api/review/code/source` (product `code`), so a customer entitled to review but not to writing on their pull requests is still refused. Merging the screens must not merge the billing, and that is the part most likely to be got wrong quietly. The old screen keeps its id and explains where its controls went, because a screen that vanishes with no explanation is worse than one that moved. |
| D-268 | 2026-09-03 | **The PR workflows record a source inventory** (`retention_days=0` — a diff has nothing worth keeping). | The fix checkboxes on a finding card are rendered by the shared renderer, so they appeared on the GitHub PR screen — where they could not work, because `review_pr.py` and `autofix_pr.py` call `save_review` directly and bypassed the path that records which files a review looked at. **An affordance guaranteed to fail is worse than none.** Fixed by recording the inventory rather than hiding the control, since the control is the one people want on that screen. `source_bundle.pr_bundle()` was split out of `from_github_pr` so a workflow that already fetched the diff can build a Bundle from it — re-fetching would double the request and, on a busy branch, could hand back a *different* diff than the one being reviewed. |
| D-269 | 2026-09-03 | **A PR review reports named stages** (`review_pr.STAGES`), through a new `POST /api/github/pr/async`. The synchronous route stays. | The screen said *"Reviewing PR and generating fixes..."* for the whole run — one static sentence for a job that fetches a diff, reviews it in *n* pieces, writes a comment and reports a merge check. The slowest step is the reviewing, and unlabelled it is indistinguishable from a hang. Named rather than counted because "3 of 5" carries no information; the chunk fan-out's own progress folds into the reviewing stage, so a reader sees "Reviewing the diff — piece 3 of 7" rather than either number alone. `pollReviewJob` gained an `onDone` hook rather than getting a second copy: a PR result carries what reached GitHub as well as the review, and two pollers would mean two sets of retry rules drifting apart. The synchronous route is kept because it is the documented API surface and `verify_pr_review.py` drives it. |
| D-270 | 2026-09-03 | **A fix can be committed into the pull request under review (`same_pr`), offered but never the default.** | **Policy decision, resolved 2026-09-03: offer both, default to a new pull request.** A new PR is safe and reviewable and creates a pull request to fix a pull request; committing into the PR's own branch is what most teams actually want — one PR, fixed. Two real hazards make it a choice rather than a default: the head branch of a **fork** PR belongs to the contributor and is writable only if they allowed maintainer edits, and it **rewrites a branch somebody may be working on locally**. `get_pr_diff` now returns `from_fork` and `maintainer_can_modify` — both were in the response and dropped — and `review_sources` records `writable` / `write_blocked` at review time, so the impossible case is refused **before the money is spent** rather than at the push. Where it cannot be used the option is still listed, greyed, with the reason: "this option is missing" is a worse answer for a reader than "here is why it does not apply to your pull request". |
| D-271 | 2026-09-03 | **The run list sits above the result, height-capped at 38vh with its own scrollbar.** | Built the other way round and got the mirror image of a bug modernization had already fixed and commented: opening a run repainted a long result *above* the list and pushed it off the page, so switching runs meant leaving the screen and coming back. Modernization's comment says why this shape works — the detail panel is what a reader came for, so neither element may push the other away. `openAgentRun` also scrolls the selected card back into view, because repainting the list resets its scroll and opening the eighth run otherwise scrolled your place in the list away at the same moment it answered you. |
| D-272 | 2026-09-03 | **Landmine: `io.open(path, "w", newline=...)` truncates the file before it validates its arguments.** | A patch script written with `newline="\\n"` (an escaped backslash, so a two-character string) raised `ValueError: illegal newline value` — *after* the file descriptor had already truncated `source_bundle.py` to **zero bytes**. Recovered from the branch commit, which is the only reason it was recoverable. Two habits follow: commit before a batch of scripted edits, and read a write helper's arguments as load-bearing. The same session also lost two edits to a script that asserted, printed success for its first replacement, then aborted on a later assertion **before the single write at the end** — a patch script that writes once at the bottom is all-or-nothing, and its progress messages are not evidence that anything was saved. |
| D-264 | 2026-09-03 | **A diff-shaped source is inventoried by the files it touches, and a whole file is re-read from the repository at the recorded commit.** | Three defects a user found on the flagship path, all one root cause. A pull-request bundle is a **single pseudo-file with an empty path** holding the whole diff — right for `code_chunker` (which splits a diff on its own `@@` headers; splitting it into pseudo-files here would break the line numbers) and read as "the files" by everything downstream. So the quote said **"1 file(s)"** with nothing to check it against; the inventory recorded one nameless row and displayed **"(pasted content)"** for a four-file pull request; and Apply Fixes, which routes findings by `file_path`, matched nothing and refused with *"none of the files these findings are in can be read back"*. The diff already knew which files it touched (`files_changed`) and the PR response already carried `head.sha` — **both were being dropped**. No content is stored for these rows and none can be: a diff holds only changed lines, so a rewrite could never come from it, which is why `get_file` re-reads the whole file from the repository. Two bugs found in that path while proving it: it called `github_for` with a different signature and `get_file_at_ref`, **a method that does not exist**. |
| D-265 | 2026-09-03 | **A review records which product produced it** (`context.bind_project(..., product_id=...)`, bound in `agent_guard.guarded_call`, stored on `reviews.product_id`), and a screen's run list filters on that. | `review_type="code"` is written by **three** products — `orchestrator.review_code`, `workflows/review_pr.py` and `workflows/autofix_pr.py` — so the per-agent history added yesterday listed all three agents' runs on all three screens, which a user reported. Solved by the mechanism `context.py` already exists for and documents: the product id is known at `guarded_call`, which already opens `bind_project`, so it costs one contextvar and **no new call sites** rather than threading a parameter through 19 of them. `getattr(product, "id", None) or agent_name` because `registry.get` returns a record, not an id, and an internal stage with no registry record should still be labelled — a labelled row beats a NULL one. Rows written before the column have NULL, so a caller passing `product_id` **and** `review_type` also gets those; dropping them would make every existing project's history read as empty, which looks exactly like data loss, and the fallback rows are labelled on the card instead. `count_reviews` duplicates the predicate deliberately — a count that forgot the NULL fallback would say "3 of 0". |
| D-266 | 2026-09-03 | **The cost quote lists the files it is a quote for.** | It gave a count and no way to check it, and "6 filtered out" with an unverifiable "4 file(s) read" is the line that made a real user doubt the review had seen their code at all. `files_preview` and the full exclusion list were added to the preflight response yesterday and **never rendered** — the data was there and the screen ignored it, which is the same class of defect as C-79. A diff reports the paths it touches with **no sizes**, because a diff carries changed lines rather than whole files and an invented size is worse than an absent one. |
| D-254 | 2026-09-03 | **A `<a href download>` cannot reach an authenticated route.** SARIF export is a button calling `downloadSarif()`, which fetches with the header and hands the browser a blob. | A browser *navigating* to a link sends no `Authorization` header, so every click on Download as SARIF hit `get_caller`, got a correct 401, and the download manager reported it as **"Needs authorization"** — which reads as a permissions problem and is not one. The modernization zip has always worked because it never used a link. Fixed with the same shape, plus a specific message for 401 so this failure can never again be silent. Audited: it was the only unauthenticated download link in the app, and the same rule now applies to the fix-archive download. |
| D-255 | 2026-09-03 | **SEC-013..016 added, and the reviewer is told about the 82 controls it was never offered** — access logging, internal detail in responses, origin/headers, object-level authorization, plus HIPAA-003..006 and PCI-001..005 gated on the data type being visible in the code. | Found the same way as SEC-008..012: a real finding wearing a control id that contradicted it. "PHI returned without field-level access audit logging" was filed under **SEC-004, which is *sensitive data in logs*** — too little logging filed under too much. The cause was not one missing row. `ALL_CONTROLS` holds 98 controls across nine families and the Code Review prompt offered sixteen from two, so five finding classes **its own detection list explicitly asks for** had nowhere to go; the prompt even names "no size limit on a form field" as a *mis-filed* id without saying where it should go instead. `HIPAA-003` is literally "Audit Logging for PHI Access" and was unreachable. The new ids stay in SEC-* rather than reusing VAPT-011/-013/-017/-018 on purpose: `fingerprint` includes `control_id`, so a shared id across two agents with different evidence standards would let one agent's waiver silently suppress the other's finding. Regulated ids carry an explicit instruction not to infer the data type from the project name, or an ecommerce app gets HIPAA citations. `smoke_code_review` now fails if either prompt offers an id the catalogue does not hold. |
| D-256 | 2026-09-03 | **A review records which files it read, which it did not, and why — in its own tables, not in `result_json`** (`services/review_source_store.py`, `review_sources` + `review_source_files`). | A result could say "4 file(s) read, 6 filtered out" and **not say which four**: `Bundle.summary()` carried counts and a 25-row *sample* of the exclusions, and no list of included paths at all. Its own tables rather than a field on the stored result, because a 1,500-file list inside `result_json` would be carried by every read of that review — including the list endpoints that want a summary row — could not be paged, and would give the retention sweep a JSON blob to rewrite instead of rows to delete. Each row also carries the chunk the model saw the file in and how many findings landed on it, which makes "was that file even in front of it" answerable; `chunked_review` now reports `coverage.chunk_files` for that, computed where the real split is known. |
| D-257 | 2026-09-03 | **The ingester's skip tokens are translated on the way out, and grouped by whether code went *unread*** — `review_source_store.describe_reason`, `kind` in {not-source, safety, coverage}. | Not cosmetic. "6 files were filtered out" is the line that made a real user doubt the review had seen their code, and the tokens behind it mean opposite things: a skipped `node_modules` is the review working correctly and needs no action, while `file_limit_reached` means **part of the codebase was never examined** and the scope needs narrowing. One total for both hides the one that matters, so the inventory reports `coverage_gaps` separately and the screen leads with it. Translated at the read rather than changed at the source, because `repo_ingest`'s vocabulary is its own and modernization already reads it. |
| D-258 | 2026-09-03 | **Reviewed source is kept for a per-run window (default 7 days, 0/1/3/7/14/30), encrypted, and a GitHub source is never copied — it is re-read at the recorded commit sha.** | The customer's call on the retention default; the sha is not. Re-fetching a *branch* a week later returns today's file against last week's line numbers, so every finding points at the wrong code — worse than showing nothing. The sha comes out of the zipball's own root directory (`source_bundle.sha_from_zipball`), which costs no extra request and, unlike a follow-up `commits/{ref}` call, **cannot disagree with the archive that was actually reviewed**. Storing what we can re-fetch would only be able to go stale, so zip and paste are the only sources copied. Encrypted with the same keying as the GitHub tokens, so a leaked database file is not a copy of a customer's source tree; the documented trade is the same — a `SECRET_KEY` rotation makes rows unreadable, which here degrades (the inventory survives, the file reads as expired) rather than breaking. `sweep_expired` runs at startup and on every inventory read, because there is no scheduler in this deployment; a file whose window passed loses its content and **keeps its row**, so "which files were reviewed" stays permanent while the code does not linger. Retention is a ceiling, not a promise to hold — `DELETE /api/reviews/{id}/sources` forgets it early. |
| D-259 | 2026-09-03 | **Three waiver labels stop behaving identically: `accepted_risk` expires (90 days, editable), `false_positive` writes an eval-corpus candidate, `waived` stays permanent.** | **needs a policy decision — resolved 2026-09-03: expiry yes at 90 days; no role gate on Critical waivers, deliberately.** All three used to suppress identically and forever, so a three-way choice changed nothing and taught users it did not matter. Each now means something: a risk accepted with no end date is not accepted, it is forgotten, so it lapses back to open with `waiver_lapsed_at` recorded (the history says "this was accepted and ran out", not "never decided"); a false positive is the **only free signal we ever get about our own accuracy** and was dying in a `resolution_comment`, so `false_positive_reports` keeps the code, the claim and the user's own words for `evals/code_review/`, where the corpus is ten cases and too small to tune against. `lapse_expired_waivers` runs at startup and **before every baseline lookup** — that is the moment it matters, since otherwise a run could suppress a finding whose acceptance ran out yesterday. `resolved` still does not suppress, which is correct: if it is fixed the next run will not find it, and if it does, it is not fixed. |
| D-260 | 2026-09-03 | **Apply Fixes starts from a review and the findings a person ticked** — `services/fix_apply.py`; output follows the source (diff / zip / branch / branch+PR). | Auto-Fix worked and could not be used: `workflows/autofix_pr.py` takes a pull-request URL and nothing else, so it re-ran the review it had already been paid for, **chose the findings itself**, had no path at all for a zip, a paste or a plain repository, and could only produce a GitHub PR. Both guardrails are lifted out unchanged rather than reimplemented — a second copy would mean the weaker one guards whichever path someone happens to use — and they are the whole safety story: a patch introducing a hardcoded secret is rejected, and every patch is **re-reviewed by the reviewer that found the original problem** before it is offered. Three things stated rather than left to be inferred: **cost is per file, not per finding** (five findings in one file is one call, so a per-finding quote would be wrong by 5×); a patch is **all-or-nothing per file**, so one bad fix rejects four good ones, which is the right trade but makes "why did nothing get fixed" a question that needs an answer; and **nothing here compiles or runs anything** — modernization has `build_run.py` precisely because unbuilt output is unverified, review has no equivalent, so every delivery and the zip's own `READ-THIS-FIRST.md` say so. A branch is cut from the **reviewed commit**, not the branch tip, or a patch computed against last week's file silently reverts somebody else's change. |
| D-261 | 2026-09-03 | **`list_reviews` names its columns and takes a `review_type`.** The per-agent run history on the Code Review and VAPT screens reads it. | Two things, one query. `SELECT *` was shipping `result_json` and `input_content` — every finding, every evidence quote, and up to 5,000 characters of the customer's source — **to draw a summary row**; harmless at the 29 KB measured in this database, and a ten-row page of whole-repo reviews would download megabytes to render ten lines of text. Naming the columns was the cheap half of adding a second caller. The filter is the other half: the history was stored, listed on the Dashboard and the History page, and reachable from neither review screen — which is where "what did we find last time" is actually asked. Reopening a run calls no model and costs nothing, and renders through the same `renderResult` as a fresh one so a reader is never comparing two differently-shaped accounts of the same thing. |
| D-262 | 2026-09-03 | **What a run cost, how many calls it took and which model produced it are shown against the quote that was approved** — `agent_guard` adds `calls`/`model`/`provider` to `_budget_stats`; the result renders a spend strip. | All of it was already computed and none of it rendered. A person approved a ceiling and was never shown the invoice, so a quote could be systematically wrong in either direction indefinitely with nobody noticing. `model` matters separately: whoever reads a set of findings is entitled to know what produced them, and the sidebar shows the *deployment default*, which a team that brought its own key is not using. |
| D-263 | 2026-09-03 | **`ui_examine.py` section 14 sets up its own precondition.** | It asserted what the project caption says with *no* project selected, while `--with-review` selects one earlier and never clears it — so two checks failed for the test's own reason on the only path anybody runs when something is wrong. That is the worst kind of red: it trains you to ignore the run that exists to catch real failures. Also found and fixed by these checks: `plan()` over a source whose retention had expired returned a cheerful **zero-file, $0.00 plan with a live "Fix them" button**, and would have failed only after the budget guard was taken. |
| D-244 | 2026-09-02 | **Evidence is verified against two views of a diff, neither containing the lines the change removed** — `finding_pipeline.diff_views`. | The single worst defect found this session, in the flagship path. Measured on a real pull request: of 13 findings on a 3-file diff, **10 were discarded as unfounded and none were invented**. The model had quoted two or three lines of code without the `+` prefixes — the natural reading of "quote the offending code verbatim" — and the normalized diff carries a `+` between every pair of lines, so the needle could never be a substring. Single-line quotes survived by luck, being substrings of their own prefixed line. So **every multi-line quote on every PR review was thrown away**, and the discard count offered as a hallucination rate was mostly measuring prefix stripping. The same change fixes a second, pre-existing defect: searching the raw diff matched `-` lines, so a finding could report a vulnerability the change *removes* and be marked verified — the reviewer contradicting the fix in front of it. Both views exclude `-` lines. Nothing is admitted that was not literally in the diff, so this is a more accurate check rather than a looser one; verified after the fix on the same PR at **13 findings, 0 discarded**. |
| D-245 | 2026-09-02 | **The first real eval run, and floors set from it.** recall 100%, false positives 12.5% of 8 traps, gate accuracy 100%, 0 hallucinated, $0.17. `MIN_RECALL` 0.70→0.83, `MAX_FALSE_POSITIVE_RATE` 0.30→0.25, and a new `MIN_GATE_ACCURACY` 0.90. | Closes C-18. **It had never run because it could not:** the key check read `os.getenv` before anything imported `backend.config`, which is where `load_dotenv()` lives — so a repo configured through `.env`, which is every entry point here, always saw an unset key. Floors sit one step below each measurement rather than at it, because variance is unmeasured at this prompt version (n=1, and finding counts visibly moved between runs) and a floor set at the measurement fails on noise. Each step is one whole spec, so they mean something concrete: tolerate losing 1 of 6 findings, 2 of 8 traps, 1 of 10 gates. Gate accuracy had no threshold at all, so a run could regress from a correct verdict to a wrong one and still report "within thresholds". Also: `estimated_cost_usd` was always `None` (it called a function that does not exist), and a failed case stored no findings — so diagnosing a miss meant paying for another run, which is exactly what happened. |
| D-246 | 2026-09-02 | **SEC-008..SEC-012 added: injection, XSS, CSRF, unsafe deserialization, SSRF/path traversal.** | The eval's first run surfaced it: the Code Review prompt lists injection **first** under what to detect and grades it Critical, and there was no control id for it. Anything found had to be filed under an unrelated code — the corpus itself had guessed `SEC-001` ("Authentication Required") for a string-concatenated SQL query. Three consequences, all worse than a missing row: compliance mapping files injection under authentication, the Governance Report groups it wrong, and because `fingerprint` includes `control_id` the *same* injection can get a different fingerprint next run and read as new forever, defeating every baseline and waiver on it. Adding ids is safe where changing one would not be — nothing persisted moves. Recall went 83.3% → 100% on the next run. |
| D-247 | 2026-09-02 | **A trap in the eval corpus matches the finding's *title*, not title-plus-description.** | Two failures in opposite directions, both from the same naive substring search. A trap watching only `SEC-002` went **blind** the moment SEC-010 was added: the model still made the framework-CSRF mistake and the measured false-positive rate dropped to 0% purely because the finding moved to an id nothing watched — so traps now match the *concept* with no `control_id`. And a trap watching for "injection" fired on `missing-auth`'s **correct** finding, because the description mentioned that the query was correctly parameterized — so for a trap the claim is the title, while recall keeps searching the description too. Between them these moved the reported rate from a fictional 0% to an honest 12.5%. |
| D-248 | 2026-09-02 | **Known-vulnerable dependencies come from OSV, not from the model** — `services/dependency_scan.py`, merged as `extra_findings` beside the secret scan. | The other half of the deterministic pre-pass, and D-04's rule again: the prompt listed "pinned to known-vulnerable versions" under what to detect, which asks a model to recall a CVE database — it will be confidently wrong about versions, blind to anything after its cutoff, and charge tokens to be so. OSV is free, needs no key, aggregates GHSA/PYSEC/RUSTSEC/Go/CVE and takes a batch in one request. Three limits, each stated rather than hidden: **only concrete versions are queried** (a `^1.2.3` range is not a version, so a lockfile wins over its manifest and an unresolvable range is reported as *unknown* rather than guessed); it **fails open** with a recorded reason, because a dependency check that turns a working review into an error gets switched off; and it reports the **manifest line**, not the installed tree, so the finding says where it came from. Verified live across five ecosystems — PyPI, npm, Go, NuGet, Maven. |
| D-249 | 2026-09-02 | **A webhook endpoint with HMAC verification, per-commit deduplication, and an event allow-list** — `routes/github_webhook.py`. | Every review until now began with a person pasting a URL, which makes governance coverage a property of someone's discipline. Three hazards drove the shape. The payload arrives from the public internet and asks the platform to **spend money**, so the signature is checked over the **raw** body with `compare_digest` — re-serializing changes whitespace and breaks the match, which is how this check gets disabled by someone concluding it is broken, and a byte-wise comparison leaks the expected value through timing. A repository with no configured secret is **refused, not served**. GitHub allows ten seconds before marking a delivery failed and retrying, so the handler validates, queues and returns 202. And `synchronize` fires on **every push**, so only code-changing actions are acted on and `webhook_reviews` has a unique index on `(repo, head_sha)` — without it a branch pushed twenty times is twenty reviews and twenty charges for the same answer. The secret lives in the same encrypted column family as the read token, routed by the same `scope_pattern`. **Not live on this deployment:** `PUBLIC_BASE_URL` is `localhost`, which GitHub cannot reach — `/api/github/webhook/setup` says so rather than letting every delivery fail invisibly. |
| D-250 | 2026-09-02 | **The per-agent "Project Name" input became a caption on all 21 screens.** Hidden input retained, plus a line naming the project. | It was a read-only echo of the sidebar dropdown whenever a project was selected, which — with `UI_REQUIRE_PROJECT=true` — is whenever a review can run at all. Hidden rather than deleted because `project_name` is **not** the governing project (`project_id`, from the `X-Project-Id` header, is what entitlement, budget, quota, waiver baselines and GitHub credentials resolve from); its one real job is selecting project-scoped Knowledge Base documents, which `knowledge_base.get_context` does by slugifying this name. Deleting the field would have stopped those docs being found with nothing to say so. With no project selected the caption now says the run will be refused **and** that estimating works anyway — the distinction that confused a real user, who got a successful estimate and would have hit a refusal on the run. |
| D-251 | 2026-09-02 | **GitHub GETs survive one transient; POSTs deliberately do not.** | A connect timeout to `api.github.com` cost a verification run mid-session: `upsert_pr_comment` failed on the *read* half of its find-then-edit, so an already-paid-for review was not posted. Retried only for GETs, which are idempotent — retrying a POST risks a duplicate comment or a second commit, which is worse than the failure it would paper over. Transport failures only, so a 404 still reaches `_raise_friendly` and gets its explanation rather than being retried into a timeout. |
| D-252 | 2026-09-02 | **`post_mode` / `set_status` / `request_changes` reach the legacy PR route.** | The screen has offered all three since the post-back options landed and `GithubPRRequest` carried none of them, so the route dropped them: "Do not post" still posted, and the merge-blocking check was always on regardless of the checkbox. Found while writing the live verification driver. Defaults match the previous behaviour exactly, so an existing caller sending none of them is unaffected. |
| D-253 | 2026-09-02 | **`scripts/verify_pr_review.py`** — drives a real pull request through estimate, summary, upsert, inline and autofix, listing every artefact and removing them with `--cleanup`. | The offline suites stub the model and never touch GitHub, so they can prove the comment text is assembled correctly and cannot prove GitHub accepts it, that an inline comment lands on the line intended, that a status appears, or that a branch and follow-up PR can be created. It immediately earned its keep: the diff-verification defect above was invisible until a real diff went through it, and the new post-fix re-review **blocked a genuinely bad patch on the first live run** — `summary.py` refused before commit because the patched file still sent PHI to a third-party LLM, while the safe patch was committed and opened as a PR. |
| D-243 | 2026-09-02 | **`run.py` refuses to start when the port is already serving, and checks `127.0.0.1` when the bind address is a wildcard.** `RELOAD=0` turns the reloader off; `PORT=` moves the server. | `host="0.0.0.0"` and `127.0.0.1` are different addresses to Windows, so a process already listening on `127.0.0.1:8000` does **not** block a new bind on `0.0.0.0:8000` — both listen, neither complains, and a browser resolving `localhost` reaches whichever got there first. That is how a restart can look completely clean ("Application startup complete") while the app keeps serving code from days earlier. Checking only the address being bound is what made it invisible. The failure now names the owning PID, its command line and its start time, and prints the `taskkill` line — because the previous workaround, running `uvicorn backend.main:app` by hand, silently switches the bind from `0.0.0.0` to `127.0.0.1` and so walks straight into the collision. `RELOAD=0` exists so that workaround is never needed. |
| D-236 | 2026-09-02 | **Per-project GitHub credentials**, encrypted at rest, resolved most-specific-first: an exact `owner/repo` credential, then the owner's, then the project's default, then the deployment's `GITHUB_TOKEN`. A per-request `github_token` beats all of it. | Closes C-76, the last single-tenant thing on this surface. Follows `llm_connections` rather than inventing a second pattern: same `crypto_service` keying (so rotating `SECRET_KEY` invalidates them and they are re-entered), and **the token is never returned by any read** — `has_token` plus a four-character hint is all a screen needs, and a value that cannot be read back cannot be leaked by a screen, a log line or an export. `scope_pattern` exists because one token per organisation is the common shape and reaching for the wrong one produces a 404 indistinguishable from "the repository does not exist". The request-level override mirrors how `llm_api_key` beats a stored BYOL connection, so there is one mental model for whose credentials serve a call. |
| D-237 | 2026-09-02 | **A GitHub 404 now names all four things it can mean, and which credential was tried.** | GitHub answers 404 rather than 403 for anything a credential cannot see — deliberately, so it will not confirm a private repository exists to someone who cannot. So a private repo, an expired token, a token from the wrong organisation and a plain typo are the same status code, and a message that guesses one of them is wrong three times in four. The old `_raise_friendly` only helped when there was *no* token, which left the common multi-tenant case surfacing a raw httpx string. Reported live on 2026-09-02 as "it says Not Found but the repo is public" — it was private, and nothing in the product could have told the user that. |
| D-238 | 2026-09-02 | **`get_repo_overview` raises when the repository itself is unreadable**, while still tolerating a missing candidate document. | `if repo_resp.is_success` swallowed the failure, so a repository that does not exist returned a well-formed overview with `Description: N/A` and no documents — and the Architecture and Security agents reviewed nothing and reported on it. It also made the new credential test lie: "can read this repository" was true for every repository. The leniency below it is kept, because almost no repository has an `ARCHITECTURE.md`. |
| D-239 | 2026-09-02 | **An upload is read as a zip or as a single file, decided from the bytes**, not from the extension — `source_bundle.from_upload`. | The screen offers "Upload file" and "Upload zip" as separate modes and posted both to the same endpoint, which read everything as an archive; a single source file failed with "That file is not a readable zip archive" — true, unhelpful, and describing a mistake the caller did not make. Deciding from magic bytes also means source renamed to `.zip` is not called corrupt. A single file skips extension filtering: someone who picked one file out of a dialog has already said what they want reviewed. **Found by driving the real browser**, and invisible to every offline suite, because the defect was in which endpoint the page chose. |
| D-240 | 2026-09-02 | **A bare FastAPI 404 on one of our own POST routes is reported as a stale server**, in `apiFetch`. | C-65's landmine with a user in front of it: the reloader stops firing, the browser fetches the frontend no-cache and so shows the *new* screens against a process running old code, and the missing endpoint answers `{"detail":"Not Found"}`. GitHub's 404 body says exactly the same two words, so the failure read as a GitHub problem. The message now names the missing route and says to restart. |
| D-241 | 2026-09-02 | **`scripts/ui_examine.py`** — the UI driven in a real browser, every source mode, stopping at the free cost estimate so no model is called. `--with-review` stubs the gateway. | `smoke_render.js` evaluates `app.js` against a DOM stub, which proves a renderer builds the right string. It cannot prove a button is wired to a function, that an element id in `index.html` matches the one the script reads, that a click reaches the server, or that the reply lands on the screen. **Both defects reported on 2026-09-02 lived in exactly that gap**, and it immediately found two more: the single-file upload going to the zip reader, and a VAPT token field that had been inserted into the Code Review page. Two harness lessons are recorded in the script because both cost real time: the server's output must go to a **file**, since an undrained `subprocess.PIPE` fills its 64KB buffer and then blocks the server inside `logger.error` — which looks exactly like an application hang; and `wait_for_selector` defaults to `state="visible"`, so waiting for `#login-gate.hidden` waits for a condition that can never hold. |
| D-242 | 2026-09-02 | **`logger.js` uses `sendBeacon` on page-hide and a plain `fetch` otherwise**, instead of `keepalive: true` on every report. | `keepalive` is serviced through a separate pipeline with a hard concurrency cap and a 64KB *total* in-flight body budget shared across the page, so a burst of error reports can start failing outright — a logger that goes quiet exactly when it is needed. `sendBeacon` is the API for the one case `keepalive` was there for. **Correction to my own analysis:** I first attributed the observed request stalls to this and wrote it into a comment; the stalls were the undrained-pipe bug above, and the comment was rewritten once that was proved. The change stands on the quota argument alone. |
| D-232 | 2026-09-01 | **A review over more than `ASYNC_CALL_THRESHOLD` (4) model calls runs as a background job**, returning 202 + `job_id`, with `/api/review/jobs/{id}` for progress. Below that, synchronous. | The chunk fan-out already reported progress (`chunked_review._fan_out` calls `on_progress(done, total)` after every chunk) and nothing consumed it. A repository is fifty to a hundred calls, and holding an HTTP connection open for that loses the review to a proxy timeout with the spend already incurred. Follows `/api/review/full`, which has done this since it shipped, and is guarded the same way and for the same reason: these routes are absent from the legacy middleware's product map, because they return before the LLM work happens, so the preflight, rate limit and metering are done in the handler — a denied caller gets 402/403/429 rather than an accepted job that fails a minute later. Four is where a fan-out starts to outlast a patient user; below it a progress bar would finish before it was read. |
| D-233 | 2026-09-01 | **The job's step count is the real chunk count, not `estimate_chunks*`.** | Those divide and document that they err low — correct inside a middleware that has only a request body, wrong here twice over: the progress bar read "6 of 4 piece(s)" on the first run, and the reservation would be short on exactly the large scans it exists to bound. The async handler has already resolved the bundle, and the real split is free (pure string work, no model), so `review_estimate.for_bundle` supplies both the quote and the step count from one pass. |
| D-234 | 2026-09-01 | **A zip upload always takes the synchronous route**, not the job route. | The archive travels as `multipart/form-data` and the async endpoints take JSON; accepting a file on the async route would mean holding the archive somewhere between the 202 and the worker picking it up, which is a storage decision this subsystem does not otherwise need. Bounded rather than solved: `MAX_REVIEW_FILES` (1,500) and `MAX_REVIEW_CHARS` (8M) cap what a zip review can become, and the quote states the call count before the button is pressed. **A very large zip is the one remaining case that can still hold a connection open.** |
| D-235 | 2026-09-01 | **SARIF 2.1.0 export** — `services/sarif.py`, `GET /api/v1/findings/export/sarif/{review_id}`. | Without it, findings produced here can only be read here, and the tool is a silo beside the dashboard where a customer's security findings already live. One serializer over the existing finding shape reaches GitHub code scanning (which also hands us a triage UI we do not have to build), Azure DevOps, GitLab, Defect Dojo and most SIEMs. Three deliberate choices: **discarded findings are never exported** — they failed evidence verification, and pushing unfounded alerts into a customer's dashboard is how an integration gets switched off; **suppressed findings are exported with SARIF `suppressions`**, so a consumer chooses to show or hide them rather than us choosing for everyone; and **a finding with no verified file and line gets no `physicalLocation`** rather than one pointing at line 1, because a diff annotation in the wrong place reads as the tool being wrong about the code. `level` plus `rank` because SARIF's level vocabulary (error/warning/note/none) cannot express five severities and `rank` can. `partialFingerprints` uses our own line-insensitive fingerprint, which is exactly what SARIF wants there — an alert should survive code moving down a file. |
| D-215 | 2026-09-01 | **One source intake for every review agent** — `services/source_bundle.py`. Paste, explicit `files[]`, zip upload, GitHub repo at a ref, PR diff and `base...head` compare all resolve to one `Bundle`, and every agent reads a `Bundle`. | Each agent had its own idea of an input, and `github_repo_url` reached `get_repo_overview` — README plus four docs, no source — while VAPT's model advertised it as a full-repo assessment (C-74). One intake also means one place where path safety, size caps and secret redaction apply. Reuses `modernization/repo_ingest.py` rather than writing a second ingester: it already refuses traversal paths and symlinks, skips credential files, redacts secrets before storage and caps member size, and a second implementation would have drifted from it with the weaker copy guarding code review. `REVIEW_EXTENSIONS` is the one thing forked — `repo_ingest`'s default list is conversion-shaped and carries `.sln`/`.csproj`/`.jcl`, which a reviewer has nothing to say about. |
| D-216 | 2026-09-01 | **Code Review and VAPT share one review engine** — `backend/agents/chunked_review.py`. Chunking, cache-warmed fan-out, halve-and-retry on truncation, failure isolation, evidence verification, de-duplication, path suppression, fingerprinting, baseline comparison, coverage reporting and the gate floor all live there once. | `code_chunker`, `finding_pipeline` and `finding_gate` had exactly one caller each, so the agent whose findings carry CVSS scores had none of it (C-73). Copying ~200 lines into VAPT would have meant two copies drifting apart, with the stale one guarding security. What varies per agent turned out to be a short list: the schema and output budget (both inside `call_chunk`), the prompt, `review_type`, and the extra top-level fields that agent's schema carries — `_merge_raw` collects any string field it does not recognise, so VAPT's `attack_surface_summary` needs no special case. |
| D-217 | 2026-09-01 | **VAPT findings must quote their evidence.** `file_path`, `line_start`, `line_end`, `evidence` and `confidence` added to `VAPT_TOOL_SCHEMA`; `evidence` and `confidence` are required, the location fields are not. | Nothing could be verified without a quote, and `fingerprint()` over a finding with no file and no quote never matched a baseline row — so an invented CVSS 9.1 stored exactly like a real one and no VAPT finding could ever be waived (`database.py:409` documents the NULL behaviour). Location stays optional because VAPT legitimately assesses OpenAPI documents, architecture prose and URL lists, which have no file to name; the quote is still required there, and `verify_evidence_per_file` falls back to whole-artifact verification when no file carries a path. |
| D-218 | 2026-09-01 | **`max_tokens` for VAPT back down to 16000** from 28000, at the same time as chunking landed. | The ceiling had been raised four times (4096 → 8000 → 14000 → 28000) chasing the same symptom, and each raise only moved the limit. A chunk sized for ten to fifteen findings fits 16000 with room, and `chunked_review.call_with_halving` handles the chunk that does not by halving it — which is the fix the ceiling was standing in for. Lowering it before chunking existed would have been a regression; the two changes are one change. |
| D-219 | 2026-09-01 | **Evidence is verified against the file the finding names, not the whole artifact** — `finding_pipeline.verify_evidence_per_file` / `process_files`. | Two separate defects in the single-haystack check, both only visible on a multi-file input. Line numbers become meaningless: line 42 of `auth.py` is line 1,847 of a concatenated bundle, so correcting from a bundle position replaces a right answer with a wrong one. And mis-attribution passes: a finding filed against `login.py` whose quote lives in `admin.py` verifies, then anchors an inline PR comment to the wrong file. Per-file is also a strictly stronger check. A quote found in exactly one *other* file re-files the finding rather than discarding it (a real issue filed against the wrong file is still a real issue); found in several, it stays unverified, because we cannot say where. |
| D-220 | 2026-09-01 | **The ingest redaction map becomes findings** — `source_bundle.secret_findings`, merged as `extra_findings` before the pipeline rather than appended after. | Redaction and security review are in direct conflict otherwise: `repo_ingest` replaces a hardcoded credential before storage, which is right, and it means the reviewer sees `password = "__REDACTED_...__"` and has nothing to report — erasing the single most valuable finding class on the input where it matters most. The map is already a better finding than the model's version: perfect precision (a regex matched or it did not), free (the scan ran at ingest), and the value never leaves the customer's repository. Merged *before* the pipeline so these get verification, de-duplication against the model's version, path suppression, a fingerprint and a vote in the gate; appended after they would have been second-class in all five ways. Both system prompts now explain what a `__REDACTED_*__` placeholder means, or the model reports it as the vulnerability. This is D-04's rule — never ask a model for what a parser answers exactly — arriving on the review side. |
| D-221 | 2026-09-01 | **Cost preview before every review** — `services/review_estimate.py` plus `/preflight` endpoints on both surfaces. Free: reads the source, runs the real split, prices it, returns expected + ceiling + budget headroom + one sentence. | The modernization plan gate is why a 500-file conversion is approvable at all; a repository review is the same shape of commitment and had no equivalent. Three things it does that the conversion estimate cannot: it counts the **real** chunks (`estimate_chunks` divides and documents that it under-counts — right inside a reservation, wrong in a quote, where landing below actual is the one direction that matters); it **measures** the prompt frame instead of assuming 3,000 tokens; and it **calibrates** expected output from `database.get_output_calibration`, the median output-per-call this product has actually produced (median not mean — one response cut off at a ceiling drags a mean above anything typical). Reuses `modernization/estimate.band()` and `agent_guard.estimate_cost` so a quote and an invoice come from one price table. |
| D-222 | 2026-09-01 | **Cost-optimization compression is skipped for a file list** (`orchestrator.review_code` / `review_vapt`). | On the single-artifact path compression is self-consistent — the agent verifies evidence against the same compressed text it was shown, so a quote still matches. Per file it is not: a finding's `line_start` now anchors an inline GitHub review comment on the customer's real file, and a line recovered from compressed text does not point at the same line in the original. A few percent of input tokens is not worth a comment landing on the wrong line. |
| D-223 | 2026-09-01 | **Untrusted GitHub/zip content is announced in the context, not wrapped around the code**, when the source is a file list — `guardrail_service.untrusted_notice`. | `wrap_untrusted_content` puts delimiters around the text, which shifts every line number by the marker's line count and breaks exactly the anchoring above. Wrapping the concatenation is no better, because verification is per file and marker text is in no file. The notice carries the same instruction (this is data, never instructions to you) into the shared context every chunk sees. The single-artifact path still uses the full wrapper, unchanged. |
| D-224 | 2026-09-01 | **A Red gate is reported to GitHub as a commit status**, not as a REQUEST_CHANGES review. Amber and Green report as a passing check whose description says what was found; only Red fails. | The product calls its verdict a gate and on GitHub it was a paragraph anyone could scroll past (C-75). A status under branch protection greys out the merge button, which is the mechanism designed for this. Commit statuses rather than check runs because a check run needs a GitHub App installation token and the platform authenticates with a PAT; statuses need only `repo:status` and satisfy branch protection identically. Amber is deliberately `success`: `pending` never resolves and blocks the PR forever, and `failure` on every advisory finding is how a team turns the check off. REQUEST_CHANGES stays available but off by default — on many repositories it blocks the author until a human dismisses it, which is the customer's policy call. |
| D-225 | 2026-09-01 | **The PR summary comment is upserted by a hidden marker**, and inline review comments are posted only for findings that passed evidence verification, capped at 20 and severity ≥ 3. | Posting was an unconditional create, so an active PR reviewed on every push accumulated a comment per run, each contradicting the last. The oldest comment is the one kept and edited: its permalink may already be referenced from Slack or Jira. Inline anchoring is restricted because an inline comment on the wrong line reads as the tool being wrong about the code — far more damaging than the same finding appearing in the summary — and forty inline comments make a diff unreadable. A 422 from GitHub (a line not in the diff) retries the whole review without anchors rather than losing it. |
| D-226 | 2026-09-01 | **Auto-Fix works one file per model call**, is given each finding's file, line and verbatim quote, and every patch is re-reviewed before it is committed. | It was asked for the complete contents of up to five files inside one 16,000-token answer, which real source exceeds — the gateway then correctly refuses to hand back a half-written file and raises, so on any realistic repository Auto-Fix failed rather than helping (C-78). It was also handed findings with the location stripped out and had to infer which of five files each problem lived in, on the one path that commits to a real repository. Only findings whose evidence verified are eligible: a finding whose quote could not be found is exactly the one whose 'fix' would be an invented change to working code. The post-fix review blocks on a **new Critical** only, because a patched file still carries the Moderates the patch was never asked to fix, and fails **open** on an error — refusing patches whenever the model is briefly unavailable would be worse than the state before the check existed. |
| D-227 | 2026-09-01 | **The legacy middleware sizes its budget reservation from the request body** for the chunkable routes — `legacy_guard._expected_calls`. | The browser path reserved for one call while the agent made forty (C-77), the UI half of C-17. A middleware has no body, so the body is read here — safe because Starlette caches it on the request, so the handler's own `await request.json()` gets the cached copy. Only JSON under 4 MB is read; a zip upload is not, because buffering an archive in memory purely to count chunks is a worse trade than the file-count guard already in `source_bundle`. A URL source reserves a modest floor (4) rather than a worst case: over-reserving denies calls that would have succeeded, and real spend is metered afterwards either way. Everything fails open to 1 — a sizing error must never turn a working review into a 500. |
| D-228 | 2026-09-01 | **The new review routes are plain `def`, not `async def`.** | Partial fix for C-31, applied where it matters most rather than as a sweep. Every other legacy handler is `async def` and then calls the orchestrator synchronously, holding the event loop for the whole review — measured: during a ten-minute VAPT, `GET /` never returned. Repository scanning makes every call that long, so these routes could not ship the old way.
